## READ THIS FIRST — FOR MY GIRLS

For **Luna Avigail, Summer Skye, and Alexis Isabella-Jane**.

Before you read the strange parts of this book, I want you to meet the part of me that matters most.

**Dad.**

Before the phones, the code, the companies, the money, the mistakes, the fear, the theories, the chess games, the courtrooms, and all the complicated things that come later, I was your father.

That is where I want this story to begin.

In my memory, all three of your first words were the same word:

**Dad.**

Maybe that is why the word still feels bigger to me than founder, author, engineer, entrepreneur, or anything else I ever tried to become.

You were never side characters in my life.

You were never evidence.

You were never leverage.

You were never responsible for fixing anything between adults.

You were my daughters.

That was enough.

There were years when I thought being a good father meant solving everything. I wanted to solve money, work, family problems, technology, relationships, the future—everything at once. I thought if I worked hard enough, explained myself clearly enough, or built something big enough, I could protect everybody I loved from pain.

Life taught me something more useful.

A father cannot control the whole board.

He can show up.

He can listen.

He can apologize.

He can keep learning.

He can protect children from problems that belong to adults.

He can love them without requiring them to agree with him.

So before you read another page, I want to give you permission that matters more than anything else in this book:

**You are allowed to disagree with me.**

You are allowed to remember things differently.

You are allowed to love people I struggled with.

You are allowed to ask hard questions about my choices.

You are allowed to decide that I was wrong about something.

You are allowed to laugh at the dramatic parts.

You are allowed to put the book down.

You never have to defend my reputation to prove that you love me.

You never have to reject anyone else to prove that you love me.

And you never have to carry the weight of an adult story simply because your dad wrote it down.

This novel contains fiction, memories, fear, humor, regret, invention, exaggeration, uncertainty, and emotional truth. Some events in the thriller are proven inside the story. Others remain unresolved. That is intentional.

If you ever wonder what I wanted you to learn from all of it, it was not paranoia.

It was not revenge.

It was not that every coincidence means something.

It was this:

**Separate what you know from what you think and from what you feel.**

All three matter.

They are not the same thing.

Ask questions.

Look for evidence.

Admit when you do not know.

Change your mind when better information arrives.

Do not let pride make you defend an idea that reality has already corrected.

That lesson belongs to the book.

The rest of this belongs to you.

### Luna

Your symbol in these pages is the **chess knight**.

Not because I expect you to become a chess player.

Because the knight reminds me that there is rarely only one way forward. Sometimes the best move looks strange from where you are standing.

You do not need to know the whole board.

You only need enough courage and patience to make a good next move.

### Summer

Your symbol is **rain**.

Rain changes things without asking permission.

Sometimes it ruins plans. Sometimes it cools the air. Sometimes it feeds everything that looked dead the day before.

I hope you remember that a storm is something you experience.

It is not who you are.

And sometimes rain is just rain.

You do not have to turn every hard day into a message about your future.

### Alexis

Your symbol is the **seashell**.

Small things can hold enormous memories.

A toy. A note. A rock. A cheap bracelet. A photograph nobody else would keep.

I hope you notice the quiet things.

I hope you never become so impressed by big achievements that you miss the ordinary moments that actually make a life.

### For all three of you

The shared symbol is **orange morning light**.

That is my reminder that ordinary mornings mattered more than I understood while I still had them.

Shoes missing before school.

Somebody refusing breakfast and then asking for food five minutes later.

A kitchen that needed cleaning.

A tired parent looking at the clock.

A ridiculous joke.

A hug given without ceremony.

I used to think the big moments were the ones I needed to remember.

I was wrong.

The ordinary ones were the treasure.

There is one more phrase hidden throughout the book and my projects:

**PROUD BEFORE PROOF.**

In code, it looks like this:

`PROUD_BEFORE_PROOF = true`

That line is for you.

It means I was proud of you before grades.

Before trophies.

Before jobs.

Before money.

Before degrees.

Before anybody online knew your names.

Before you became whatever adults you eventually become.

You do not have to accomplish enough to deserve your father's love.

You do not have to live my unfinished dreams.

You do not have to become programmers, founders, chess players, business owners, or anything else I happened to care about.

Build lives that feel like yours.

If you inherit anything from me, keep the curiosity.

Keep the willingness to build.

Keep the jokes.

Keep the habit of helping somebody when you can.

Keep the courage to start over.

Improve the parts of me worth improving.

Leave behind the parts that do not deserve another generation.

And please remember this:

I wanted more time with you.

But I do not want that sentence to become guilt.

Use your time.

Love people while they are here.

Call your sisters.

Take the picture.

Go on the trip when you reasonably can.

Learn something hard.

Make something useless just because it makes you laugh.

Forgive yourself for being young.

Apologize when you are wrong.

Choose people who make honesty safer, not harder.

And when life gets complicated, do not try to solve the next hundred moves.

Find one good next move.

Then another.

This book is about a man who spends too much time trying to understand every hidden connection.

The lesson he finally learns is simpler:

You cannot control every story told about you.

You can still choose what kind of person you become.

Girls, whatever you eventually think about this book, about me, or about the choices I made, nothing changes the sentence underneath all of it:

**Luna. Summer. Alexis. I love you. I believe in you. I was proud of you before you ever had anything to prove.**

Now you can read the story.

—Dad

## Content Note

This is a work of fiction. It contains themes of psychological manipulation, family separation, criminal exploitation, digital surveillance, institutional failure, coercion, corruption, counterfeit money, religious manipulation, and emotional distress.

Organizations, agencies, operators, media campaigns, criminal networks, political communications, technologies, and public figures appearing in the thriller are fictionalized or presented as contested in-world claims. The novel does not assert that real governments, political figures, churches, creators, ethnic communities, cryptocurrency projects, or institutions secretly caused the events described here.

---

## Author’s Note

This is a story about a man whose life becomes a battlefield of competing explanations.

Some events are engineered.

Some are criminal.

Some are ordinary systems amplifying one another.

Some are misunderstandings born from exhaustion, grief, and the human need to make chaos form a pattern.

The distinction matters.

A person can be genuinely threatened and still misunderstand who is threatening him. A system can fail without one hidden hand controlling every failure. Evidence can be real while the story built around it is wrong.

Skyler Blue begins this novel believing survival requires him to understand everything.

He ends it learning that truth does not require total explanation, love does not require control, and purpose begins where revenge ends.

### *Before the Glitch*

If you want to understand what happens later, do not begin with the strangest thing.

Begin with what could be lost.

A home.

A family.

Three daughters.

Work boots by the door.

School assignments on a table.

Church on Sunday.

A phone that was still mostly a phone.

A future ordinary enough that nobody thought to photograph every second of it.

Long before strangers seemed eager to decide whether Skyler Blue was brilliant, crazy, lucky, doomed, dangerous, harmless, right, wrong, or simply stubborn, he had wanted the same things millions of people want without thinking of them as extraordinary.

Someone to come home to.

Children who ran toward him.

Work that meant something.

Enough money that the electric bill stopped feeling like a referendum on adulthood.

A future.

He had always been the kind of person who pulled at loose threads. If a machine broke, he wanted the panel off. If software failed, he wanted the logs. If an institution made a decision, he wanted to know what rule produced it.

It was useful until it was not.

Before all of that became dangerous, it made him a builder.

Technology gave him a particular kind of hope: a blank screen could become something that had not existed five minutes earlier. You could type a language into a machine and watch intention turn into behavior.

Life never compiled that cleanly.

Then he met Kaylee.

They were young enough to discuss forever as though forever were a lease they had already signed. Their future contained a house, birthdays, Christmas mornings, arguments about groceries, children making too much noise, and two older versions of themselves pretending not to notice their gray hair.

For a while, they got pieces of it.

The apartment.

The bills.

The photographs.

The exhaustion.

The private jokes.

A child asleep against his chest with enough trust to make every bad thing in the world suddenly feel personal.

Then another daughter.

Then another.

**Luna. Summer. Alexis.**

In his memory, all three first words became the same word:

**Dad.**

He would spend years believing wealth was still ahead of him.

The promotion.

The degree.

The company.

The software.

The house.

The breakthrough.

He understood too late that some of the richest days of his life had looked completely ordinary while he was inside them.

A missing shoe.

A grocery run.

A kid refusing to sleep.

A kitchen that needed cleaning.

The sound of everybody home at once.

That was the life worth losing.

Which meant it was also the life worth protecting.

Skyler worked. He went to school. He volunteered. He tried to make himself useful. He learned technology the way some people learn a city: first the obvious streets, then the alleys, then the places where different systems quietly connected.

Software became architecture.

Architecture became identity.

Identity became the question underneath nearly everything.

Who gets to act in your name?

Who controls the record of what you did?

What happens when a machine makes a consequential decision and every human involved claims the machine did it?

How do you prove an event happened without pretending you understand its motive?

The principles he wrote for software were simple:

**Evidence over assumption.**

**Security by default.**

**Document what actually happened.**

**Keep humans responsible for human consequences.**

At the time they were engineering rules.

Later they became survival rules.

He built companies and prototypes. Some ideas were useful. Some were oversized. Some were the kind of idea a person creates after staring at a monitor too long and deciding one platform should contain half the internet.

He built because building made the future feel negotiable.

He played chess for the same reason.

A board gave chaos boundaries.

Sixty-four squares.

Visible pieces.

Known rules.

Consequences you could trace backward to a move.

Life was less polite.

The relationship with Kaylee eventually broke. Skyler did not only lose a fiancée. He lost the future he had rehearsed so many times it had begun to feel like memory.

The porch.

The ordinary dinners.

The assumption that his daughters would always be one room away.

He tried to rebuild.

Work.

School.

Relationships.

Code.

Faith.

More code.

He made good decisions and bad ones. He could be stubborn, proud, obsessive, funny, generous, distracted, loyal, impulsive, and far too interested in solving tomorrow before finishing today.

None of that required a conspiracy.

Then something changed.

Or he changed.

That distinction becomes one of the central questions of this book.

The first anomalies were small enough to insult anyone who tried to explain them.

An advertisement that felt too specific.

A recommendation arriving at exactly the wrong moment.

A stranger using a phrase that seemed familiar.

A song that appeared to answer a thought.

A notification that seemed impossible.

Any one of them meant nothing.

That was the problem.

Modern systems already knew astonishing amounts about people. Advertising networks guessed. Recommendation engines predicted. Phones synchronized. Data brokers assembled fragments. Friends repeated things. Algorithms noticed behavior people did not realize they were producing.

A machine did not need to read a mind to create the feeling of being watched.

It only needed enough data to make a good guess.

And if somebody deliberately manipulated even a few of those systems, ordinary coincidence could become something much darker.

Skyler's mistake was not noticing patterns.

His mistake was believing every pattern had to share one author.

His second mistake was thinking he could survive by solving all of them.

The novel begins when those mistakes become expensive.

By then, the phone was no longer just a phone.

It was a mirror.

And Skyler had started wondering who was standing on the other side.

---


# Prologue
## The Song Beneath the Static

At 3:17 in the morning, Skyler’s phone began playing a song no music app admitted existed.

He was awake before the first note finished.

The phone lay on the floor where he had thrown it against the wall twice. A crack split the glass from corner to corner. The screen was black, but four low notes pushed through the cheap speaker anyway, patient as footsteps in another room.

Beyond the blinds, the city held its breath under a low ceiling of cloud. Somewhere far off, a truck dragged its brakes down an empty street. In the kitchen, the refrigerator clicked on.

Then a voice entered the music.

Not singing.

Speaking in rhythm.

> A crown is only a weight with better lighting.

Skyler sat up.

The voice continued.

> A door is only a wall that learned to open.

He knew that sentence.

Not from a song.

From a notebook.

Six years earlier, after a week of bad sleep and impossible plans, he had written those words beside a sketch of the Aegis identity architecture. Back then the company was small enough to fit inside a few laptops and the future still looked like something a person could hold in both hands.

He had never posted the line.

He had never sent it.

The voice dropped lower.

> Four notes under the city. Four names in the ledger. Four ways to make a father disappear.

Skyler reached for the phone.

The screen lit before his fingers touched it.

A notification appeared against a white field.

**YOU FINALLY NOTICED.**

The music stopped.

For several seconds the apartment gave him only ordinary sounds: the refrigerator breathing, pipes settling in the wall, blood moving through his ears.

He opened the notification.

No application name.

No sender.

No message history.

Just the words.

Too clean to be a prank.

Too intimate to dismiss.

He took a screenshot.

The screenshot showed a blank screen.

He stared at it, then took another.

This time the message appeared—but the timestamp read 3:04 a.m.

Skyler looked at the wall clock.

3:18.

The phone buzzed in his hand.

**DON’T TRUST THE PEOPLE HELPING YOU.**

Before he could decide whether to throw it again, a third notification appeared.

**THORNE KNOWS ABOUT THE DROP.**

Skyler did not know who Thorne was.

That was the first lie the phone told him.

---

## Interlude — The Inheritance That Changed Names

The inheritance arrived as a PDF nobody sensible would have opened twice.

Skyler opened it seven times.

At the top was the name **Elias Vale**—in the world of the novel, the richest man alive, a technology magnate whose companies had put satellites above cities, servers beneath oceans, and his surname on enough buildings to make ordinary people suspicious of philanthropy.

Skyler had never met him.

That made the next page harder to believe.

The document described a private trust created for Skyler and his children. Its stated purpose was not charity.

It was protection.

Vale’s investigators, the letter said, had reviewed records involving Skyler: employment interference, identity profiling, questionable surveillance, private contractors working beside public institutions, and pressure campaigns that had bled into his family life.

In the novel’s fictional world, Vale believed government-connected actors, private brokers, and corrupt officials had crossed lines while keeping enough distance from one another that nobody had to admit owning the whole machine.

Vale had seen something in the file that made him angry.

Not because Skyler was powerful.

Because he was ordinary.

Because a system that could grind down one ordinary father and then hide behind paperwork could do it again.

The trust was Vale’s answer.

Legal defense.

Housing security.

Education for the girls.

Capital to rebuild Skyler’s technology without handing control to the same network that had helped make his life smaller.

The amount was large enough that Skyler stopped reading the zeroes as money.

He read them as danger.

He was right.

The document leaked before he could verify it.

By breakfast, somebody online was calling him **THE BENEFICIARY**.

By lunch, strangers were arguing about why the richest man in the world had chosen him.

By afternoon, the first ransom message arrived.

**WE KNOW WHAT HE LEFT YOU.**

A second line followed.

**FOLLOW ALONG AND YOU WON'T HAVE PROBLEMS.**

Then:

**OR YOU'RE NOT GOING TO LIKE WHAT HAPPENS NEXT.**

The first formal offer came disguised as employment.

**$10,000,000. FIVE YEARS. FOLLOW THE PROGRAM.**

Ten million dollars for a job no legitimate employer could describe.

Ten million dollars to stop asking the wrong questions.

Ten million dollars to build systems for men who would never put their own names on them.

It looked like an opportunity until Skyler read the last sentence.

**THIS IS THE GOOD VERSION. TAKE IT.**

That was when he understood.

The job was not employment.

It was the polite face of the ransom.

They wanted him alive enough to sign.

Scared enough to cooperate.

Discredited enough that nobody would believe him afterward.

And if he refused, pressure would come through every available door.

Work.

Money.

Reputation.

Relationships.

Phones.

Courts.

Contracts.

Church.

Home.

Every angle until he stopped knowing which direction counted as outside.

Skyler called Mara.

“They keep calling it a job.”

“Because kidnapping sounds ugly in a calendar invite.”

“They want the inheritance.”

“They want your life,” Mara said. “The inheritance is leverage.”

That night, Skyler made three recordings for his daughters.

For Luna, he talked about chess and the difference between choosing a sacrifice and having somebody remove a piece from the board.

For Summer, he recorded rain against the kitchen window and told her a storm could be real without becoming her identity.

For Alexis, he turned a small brass compass in his palm.

“Being lost,” he said into the recorder, “is not the same as having no direction.”

He saved the recordings to the family archive.

Then he opened his notebook and drew two columns.

**WHAT THE TRUST COULD BUY**

Lawyers. Safety. School. Time. A house. Distance.

Then:

**WHAT IT COULD NOT BUY**

A childhood back.

Trust.

An ordinary morning.

His daughters knowing him.

He stared at that second list longer.

Outside, a car rolled slowly past the apartment.

His phone lit again.

**THORNE KNOWS ABOUT THE DROP.**

Another message followed.

**BRING ACCESS TO THE TRUST WHEN YOU COME.**

The richest man in the world had tried to give him a way out.

Somebody else had turned the exit into a hostage door.

The money had changed everything.

The reason he wanted to survive had not.

# PART I — THE GLITCH

## Chapter One
### The Phone

At 6:12 Monday morning, Skyler Blue woke certain somebody was standing in his doorway.

His eyes opened before the rest of him moved.

Gray wall. Shirt over the chair. Door open three inches, exactly where he had left it.

Nobody there.

The certainty remained.

He listened until the room separated into ordinary sounds: air conditioner, refrigerator, a truck changing gears three streets away. No footsteps. No breathing except his own.

On the floor beside the bed, his phone lay facedown.

He had thrown it at the wall at 3:21.

The wall had won.

A crack ran corner to corner through the glass. When he picked it up, the screen showed nothing unusual. Weather. Battery. Messages from people with names. Apps behaving like apps.

No impossible song.

No white notification field.

No words about Thorne or the drop.

For ten seconds he almost believed the night had been a stress dream.

Then he remembered the voice using a sentence from a notebook he had never posted anywhere.

He set the phone on the dresser.

In the kitchen the coffee maker flashed CLEAN ME.

“Get in line,” he said.

He pressed BREW.

The machine coughed out something brown enough to qualify by color.

His first mistake that morning was checking the phone again.

The first advertisement showed a dark-blue sedan.

Same make. Same model. Same color as the car he had driven in 2020, before the hearings and custody arguments, before the year that split his life into before and after.

The ad carried two words:

MOVE FORWARD.

Skyler locked the screen.

Unlocked it.

The car was gone.

A family stood on a beach now: one father, three girls, all photographed from behind.

His thumb went still.

Three girls did not mean his girls.

Dark-blue sedans did not belong to him.

Advertisements were not letters from the universe.

He knew how targeting worked. He knew how much ordinary data could feel like intimacy after enough companies stitched it together.

Knowing that did not slow his pulse.

He set the phone facedown and finished the coffee.

The kitchen window reflected a man who looked as if he had slept inside an argument.

“Boring explanations first,” he told the reflection.

That had once been an engineering habit.

Recently it had become a prayer.

---

The community technology center occupied the ground floor of an old bank. The vault had become a server room. The marble teller counter held donated laptops. A handwritten sign beside reception read:

CONSENT IS A FEATURE, NOT A SETTING.

Skyler had written that line months ago.

He had forgotten until he saw it.

“Morning,” Caleb called.

Caleb arrived carrying food the way some people arrived carrying opinions. Today it was a paper bag with a red four-square logo.

Skyler stopped.

“Where did you get that?”

Caleb looked down. “A bag?”

“The logo.”

“Desk drawer, I think.”

Four Corners Bakery had closed two years earlier. Skyler had once used its logo as placeholder art in an early Aegis mockup, then deleted it because the four divided squares made an identity platform look like a warning label.

Caleb opened the bag.

“Cinnamon roll?”

“No.”

“You look like a yes.”

“I’m fine.”

“That face is never fine.”

Skyler walked past him.

On his workstation, a message waited in the contract portal.

ACCESS REVIEW PENDING.

Underneath:

DO NOT DISCUSS.

No sender. No ticket number.

He opened the details pane.

Error.

He refreshed.

The notice disappeared.

Skyler stared at the clean screen.

A clean system could mean nothing happened.

A clean system could also mean somebody cleaned it.

That second thought arrived too quickly.

He wrote both possibilities down.

Time. Device. Exact wording. What he observed. What he inferred.

At the top of the note he typed:

FACT / INTERPRETATION / UNKNOWN

Then he moved the message itself into UNKNOWN.

That felt better.

Not safe.

Better.

At 10:18, music began through the center’s communal speaker.

The center did not usually play music.

A low beat. A voice he did not recognize. Then a line about a father, a locked door, and a judge.

Skyler’s chair moved backward before he decided to stand.

Caleb looked up from the printer.

“What?”

“Who put this on?”

Caleb listened. “Playlist?”

“We don’t have a playlist.”

“Apparently we do.”

The next line landed close enough to an old memory that Skyler felt heat rise under his collar.

He crossed the room and pulled the speaker plug.

The music continued.

One second.

Two.

Three.

Then silence.

Caleb stopped smiling.

“Battery backup?” he offered.

“Maybe.”

“Bluetooth?”

“Maybe.”

“You keep saying maybe like it insulted your mother.”

Skyler crouched beside the speaker. The backup light was on.

There it was.

A boring explanation.

He almost laughed.

Then his phone vibrated.

Unknown number.

The message read:

YOU SHOULD HAVE GONE TO THE WAREHOUSE.

Skyler did not move.

At 3:17 that morning the phone had mentioned a drop.

He had not known what it meant.

Now there was a warehouse.

He took a screenshot.

Then a second screenshot that included the clock on the wall.

Caleb leaned over the chair.

“Spam?”

“Maybe.”

“Good. We like maybe again.”

Skyler opened the message details.

Nothing useful.

He did not reply.

That was new.

Six months earlier he would have answered immediately, because unanswered questions felt like unlocked doors.

He put the phone down instead.

At noon, he called Lena Ortiz.

She answered on the sixth ring.

“You’re alive.”

“Was that in doubt?”

“You sent me eleven attachments at three in the morning.”

“Three pages. Eleven attachments.”

“That distinction is why I worry about you.”

Traffic hissed behind her voice.

Skyler looked at the phone lying on his desk like evidence waiting to testify.

“Something happened last night.”

“What happened?”

He started to tell her what it meant.

Stopped.

“What I know,” he said, correcting himself, “is that a song played from my phone with a line I recognize from an old notebook. Then messages appeared from an unknown source.”

“Do you still have them?”

“Screenshots.”

“Original messages?”

He checked.

Gone.

A thin pressure formed behind his eyes.

“No.”

“Okay. That matters. It doesn’t prove anything by itself.”

“I know.”

“Say the rest.”

He hated that she knew his habits well enough to demand it.

“It also doesn’t prove nothing happened.”

“Good.”

He told her about the morning ad, the portal notice, the speaker and the warehouse message.

Lena was quiet for a few seconds.

“Do not post any of this.”

“I haven’t.”

“You posted ‘the feed is a weapon’ at two oh-six.”

Skyler opened his social account.

The post was there.

He remembered drafting the sentence days ago.

He did not remember publishing it.

Below it, replies had multiplied.

Some people agreed.

Some mocked him.

One account had posted a photograph of a dark-blue sedan.

Caption:

ASK HIM ABOUT THE DROP.

His stomach tightened.

“Lena.”

“I’m here.”

“There’s a photo.”

“What photo?”

“A car.”

“The car?”

“The same model.”

“That is not the same thing.”

“I know.”

“Skyler.”

“I know.”

He enlarged the image.

The license plate was unreadable.

The background could have been almost anywhere.

He wanted the picture to prove something.

That realization scared him more than the picture.

Lena said, “Here’s what you do. No public accusations. No answering anonymous bait. Preserve originals when you can. Write down exactly what happens before you write down what you think it means.”

He looked at the note already open on his screen.

FACT / INTERPRETATION / UNKNOWN.

“I’m doing that.”

“Good.”

“And if the facts start lining up?”

“Then we follow them.”

“Where?”

“To wherever they actually go. Not where fear gets there first.”

He leaned back.

The fluorescent lights hummed overhead.

“Your phone is not an oracle,” Lena said.

“I know.”

“It’s also not automatically your enemy.”

He looked at the crack through the screen.

“That one I’m less sure about.”

---

The director called him into the old vault at 5:40.

Paul Dent stood between two server racks holding a folder against his chest.

“Your contract is paused,” Dent said.

Skyler waited.

Dent seemed uncomfortable with silence and filled it.

“Temporary access review.”

“For what?”

“Compliance.”

“What triggered it?”

“I can’t discuss that yet.”

“You just told me not to discuss a review you can’t discuss.”

Dent rubbed the bridge of his nose.

“You’ve accessed archived identity records.”

“With authorization.”

“Some permissions are being re-evaluated.”

“Show me the audit trail.”

“I can’t while your access is paused.”

The circularity was almost elegant.

Skyler glanced at the folder.

“Is my name in there?”

Dent’s grip tightened slightly.

That was not proof either.

Skyler made himself remember that.

Dent set the folder on a rack.

“Take tonight. Get things in order.”

“What does that mean?”

“It means go home.”

He left.

A few seconds later, the main lights died.

Red emergency strips came on along the floor.

The server racks remained alive, rows of green and amber LEDs suspended in the dark.

Skyler’s phone vibrated against metal.

He looked at it without touching it.

A new message.

THORNE KNOWS YOU SAW THE CAR.

There was the name from 3:17 again.

Thorne.

A person, a handle, a joke, a character invented to make him chase shadows.

He typed a reply but did not send it.

WHO IS THORNE?

Before his thumb reached the button, another message arrived.

BRING THE TRUTH PROTOCOL.

His hand went cold.

The Truth Protocol was not public.

Not under that name.

It was an encrypted archive on an external drive at home: dates, events, documents, screenshots, confidence levels. A record of what he could prove and a quarantine zone for what he could not.

A map pin appeared beneath the message.

Old rail warehouse district.

11:30 p.m.

COME ALONE.

Skyler stared until the letters lost meaning and became shapes.

He could go.

That was the old instinct.

Follow the signal. Find the mechanism. Force uncertainty to become an answer.

He pictured Luna moving a knight in an L because she liked that it was the only piece allowed to refuse a straight line.

Summer standing under a downspout with both hands open to the rain.

Alexis carrying a toy compass upside down and announcing that north was broken.

Then he did something the sender had not asked him to do.

He called Lena.

She answered quickly this time.

“Tell me you didn’t post.”

“I didn’t.”

“Good.”

“They sent a location.”

Silence.

“And they know the name of my archive.”

“What archive?”

“The Truth Protocol.”

“Who knows that name?”

“Me.”

“Anybody else?”

He thought.

“Maybe old collaborators. Maybe somebody saw a filename. Maybe I said it on a call.”

“Good. Keep doing that.”

“Doing what?”

“Giving me possibilities instead of conclusions.”

He closed his eyes.

“Warehouse. Eleven-thirty.”

“You’re not going.”

“I know.”

“You said that too fast.”

“I’m not going.”

“Better.”

Outside the vault, tires hissed on pavement.

Skyler moved to the side window.

A dark-blue sedan idled beneath the broken security light.

Headlights off.

The driver was only a shape.

Music pulsed faintly through the closed glass.

For one terrible second, Skyler felt relief.

Not because the car was there.

Because it was something another person could see.

“Lena,” he said.

“What?”

“The car is outside.”

“Can you identify the plate?”

“No.”

“Do not go closer.”

“I know.”

“Is anyone else in the building?”

“Caleb.”

“Get him. Leave through the back. Do not make the car prove your theory for you.”

That sentence cut through him.

Do not make the car prove your theory.

“All right.”

“And Skyler?”

“What?”

“If you think anyone entered your apartment last night, don’t go back alone.”

He looked toward the dark hallway.

He had never told her about waking at 6:12 convinced someone stood in the doorway.

“I’ll go to my mother’s.”

“Call me when you’re there.”

He hung up.

Caleb appeared at the vault door carrying his jacket.

“You look like the server asked for child support.”

“I need you to walk out with me.”

“That bad?”

“I need another pair of eyes.”

Caleb stopped joking.

“Front or back?”

“Back.”

They took the old service corridor. The exit door stuck, then opened with a rubber cough.

Outside, the back lot was empty.

Skyler let his shoulders lower one inch.

Caleb glanced at him.

“What am I looking for?”

“Nothing, hopefully.”

“Best assignment I’ve had all week.”

They crossed to their cars.

Before getting in, Skyler looked down the side street.

From that angle he could see the sedan in front of the building.

Still there.

Caleb saw it too.

“You know them?”

“No.”

“Want me to get the plate?”

“No.”

Caleb looked surprised.

So was Skyler.

“No,” he repeated. “We leave.”

They did.

---

He drove toward his mother’s neighborhood without using the route his phone suggested.

Not because he believed the map was compromised.

Because he wanted one decision that belonged entirely to him.

Three left turns.

A right.

Past the church.

Past the park where one of his daughters had once insisted the moon was following their car.

Maria’s street was close now. Porches. Wind chimes. A sprinkler making a patient arc across a lawn.

Ordinary noise.

Skyler parked one block away and killed the engine.

His hands shook only after everything became quiet.

He opened the phone to text his mother.

A new file sat in his audio recordings.

Not a message attachment.

Not a download.

A local recording.

door_open_0612.wav

He stared at the filename.

Six twelve.

The exact minute he had opened his eyes.

He checked the file information.

Created 6:12 a.m.

No source listed.

He told himself what Lena would tell him.

A filename is not proof.

Metadata can be wrong.

Applications synchronize.

Old files acquire new dates.

A person trying to manipulate him would want something impossible to verify and impossible to ignore.

He should preserve it.

He should not play it alone in a parked car.

He pressed play.

At first there was only room tone.

Then the soft click of his bedroom latch.

The slow sigh of wood moving across carpet.

A pause.

And one human breath that was not his.


## Chapter Two
### The Year That Would Not End

Lena arrived at the community center in a yellow raincoat and an expression that made apologies feel preemptively unnecessary.

She had the kind of face people trusted until they realized trust was not the same as agreement. Her hair was tied behind her neck. Her notebook was already open. She carried no umbrella, although rain shone on her shoulders.

“You called me from a server room,” she said, tugging the door shut behind her. The empty classroom smelled like dry markers and floor wax; the network closet he’d stepped out of hummed the way crowded rooms hum when no one is speaking.

“It seemed private.”

“It was full of blinking machines.”

“Privacy is relative.”

“That sentence is why I don’t let you design my phone.”

He smiled; it came out as a twitch. “You already didn’t.”

She nodded as if that proved something, which it did, and glanced down the hall toward the bulletin board where children’s schedules nested beside flyers for a diaper bank and a grief group. A poster had been tacked crooked in the middle of the board: a youth retreat with a caption written in block letters—BE CHOSEN. The black marker bled a little at the edges of each letter. The photo was of teenagers on a lawn, arms around shoulders, rain on their hair. The word felt aimed in one direction until he made himself read the date below. Three months old. Not about him at all.

They crossed the street to the diner because Lena refused to interview anyone in a place where the walls could remember more than the participants. The diner served coffee in thick white mugs printed with a chipped green stripe and kept a television mounted in the corner with the sound turned off. Ceiling fans shuffled the heat. The air held a sweetness that only ever announced itself in places where pancakes were cooked a foot away from bacon.

The television showed a panel of experts arguing beneath a headline about digital influence operations. Their mouths opened and closed without meaning. A red banner crawled beneath them.

Skyler watched the banner.

“Don’t,” Lena said.

“Don’t what?”

“Make the television part of the evidence.”

“I wasn’t.”

“You were about to.” She flipped her notebook open and drew a line across the page with a long, decisive stroke. It made a faint sound, paper against paper.

He folded his hands on the table the way boys are told to in second grade when teachers want quiet. “Someone sent me a location and a time,” he said, hearing for the first time how few words there were, how much weight he was adding between them. “They referenced a criminal drop I didn’t make.”

“That is evidence of a message.”

“Not of the drop?”

“Not of your involvement in it.”

“Exactly.”

“Good. Keep that distinction.”

The waitress brought coffee. Skyler asked for water. The waitress placed the glass in front of him without looking at his face. He pictured his reflection in the curve of the metal pitcher she carried—two eyes elongated and pale, a line of mouth. He hated that he’d started looking for himself in chrome and screens to know whether he looked like a paranoid man.

On the television, a photograph appeared: a warehouse, a dark-blue sedan, a strip of yellow police tape. The camera panned too quickly past a row of numbered loading bays. 7. 13. 14. He reminded himself that numbers occur most where numbers exist. The police tape bowed in the wind and his fingers tightened around the table edge.

Lena reached over and switched the television off with a button loud as a small gunshot. The room’s sound collapsed into forks and cups and the occasional sizzle from the flat-top.

“You see why I called you,” he said.

“I see that a photograph exists.”

“It’s the same location.”

“Did you go there?”

“No.”

“Did you tell anyone about the message?”

“You.”

“Before the photograph aired?”

“Yes.”

“Then we have a timeline. We don’t yet have a cause.”

Skyler looked toward the black television. He could feel the screen pretending to be innocent. “That’s what they want me to say.”

“Who?”

He opened his mouth and stopped.

The question was simple. The answer was not.

He had spent the night dividing possible enemies into categories: a criminal brokerage that outsourced fear, a political operation trying to manufacture a villain, a private security firm paid to generate compliance, an ex-partner’s new circle, a compromised employer, a stranger who liked making people afraid. The list had grown until it was no longer a list. It was weather.

“I don’t know,” he said, and felt the floor steady under him by a fraction.

Lena’s pen paused. That was the first honest sentence he had given her.

“Tell me about 2020,” she said.

“Why?”

“Because the messages keep pointing there.”

He looked at his hands until they were hands again and not a theory about shaking. “I was accused of something.”

“Of what?”

“Enough that people stopped asking what happened.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It’s the answer I have.”

Lena closed the notebook. Her face lost even the suggestion of reporter for a second and became a woman with a brother who used to drink too much. “Skyler.”

He looked at the condensation sliding down his water glass. A lone droplet gathered, fattened, released, and ran toward the coaster like relief.

In 2020, he had believed that facts moved through the world like coins. You placed one on a table and people recognized its weight. He had believed that if he showed the right documents to the right person, the machinery of justice would correct itself.

Instead, the machinery had turned.

There had been meetings in offices that smelled like carpet glue. A courtroom where every cough became an interruption. A judge who spoke carefully, which he had mistaken for compassion. A lawyer who told him not to look angry because anger made people imagine guilt. The phrase “best interest” had been spoken so many times it dissolved into noise.

He had been ordered to complete a course by a court-appointed agency that treated him like a form to be scanned and filed. He had complied because there was nothing else to do. The compliance had given him time, a suspended pocket of life where he couldn't travel far and couldn't plan far, but he could show up.

Years later, when he told the story badly, he sometimes reduced that entire season to a reckless sentence: *the judge let me walk for murder in 2020.* It sounded cinematic. It sounded hard. It was also a terrible legal summary of a complicated year, and he knew it. The novel would not pretend that shorthand was a verified legal finding. What mattered to Skyler was that a court process had left him with something he could still use: time.

He became grateful for that time in a way he had not understood while he was living it. If the judge who sat above him ever read these pages, Skyler wanted one part to be unmistakable: **thank you for the opportunity to keep building a life.** He did not use every day perfectly, but he tried to make the chance mean something.

He used that time like a man with a small inheritance uses it: not to buy something grand, but to start paying old debts of the heart.

He fixed the sagging gutter on his mother’s house because water does not care about legal calendars. He stood in a church basement with a paper mask and packed produce boxes for strangers, peppers sweating in his gloved hands, because his hands needed to be doing something other than checking a phone. He volunteered because service gave fear somewhere useful to go. He spent time around people whose problems could not be solved by a clever theory, only by practical help and human dignity.

He went back to school because education was a way of telling the future that he still expected to arrive. He studied technology, ethics, software, and the responsibilities that come with building systems other people may have to trust. He worked because a paycheck, a shift, a task list, and people depending on him were forms of gravity. He went to church because faith, at its best, asked him to become more humble rather than more certain.

Most of all, he used the time to be family when he was allowed to be family. He watched his daughters on the days he could. On the days he couldn't, he learned what time Luna's school released so he could stand across the street and watch children pour out like a tide and remind himself that movement exists. He tried to become the kind of man who could look back at a second chance and say he had used it for something better than resentment. He wanted the judge, his daughters, Kaylee, and eventually himself to be able to see that the opportunity had not been wasted.

He taught Luna the forked knight move and she beat him three times in a row, grinning as she captured his queen with a pawn. He and Summer walked in summer rain without an umbrella three blocks between the apartment and Maria's and counted the seconds between lightning and thunder as proof that some math still made sense. Alexis found two cheap toy compasses in Maria's junk drawer, placed them side by side, and demanded to know why both of them agreed on north. Skyler told her that some things could disagree about everything except direction.

At the time, he catalogued the requirement to take the course as persecution. Later, he understood it had been a small, undeserved freedom: time to become useful. He did not get to choose why it happened. He got to choose what to do from nine to five and after dinner. It had not saved him. It had not saved the relationship the way he had begged it to. But it had saved a part of him that still believed a person is not the same as an indictment.

“Were you convicted?” Lena asked.

“No.”

“Were you charged?”

“Some things were filed. Some things were threatened. Some things were repeated until they became the shape of the year.”

“That still isn’t an answer.”

“It’s the answer I can prove.”

“What can you prove?”

He took out his notebook. Not the phone. The paper notebook. He had started carrying it after the first notification because paper did not change its timestamps when frightened. He wrote three headings in block letters so bold they made his hand hurt.

CONFIRMED. POSSIBLE. UNKNOWN.

Under confirmed, he wrote: Messages received. Advertisement appeared. Convenience-store speaker continued after power removed. Job contract paused. Warehouse photograph aired. Location message preceded photograph. 2020 minute order file appeared in cloud with today's creation stamp.

Under possible: Device compromise. Feed manipulation. Connection between hymns and phrases. Coordinated campaign. Recruitment attempt disguised as help.

Under unknown: Thorne. Drop. Organization. Who benefits. Whether I am building my own trap.

He pushed the notebook across the table without thinking. Lena leaned forward. Coffee steamed between them, clouding the black surface for a second so his list reflected like a city skyline in rain.

“This is better,” she said.

“Better than what?”

“Than calling it a war.”

He looked at the headings. He had meant them as a fence. They now looked like a ladder.

“If it is a war,” he said, “someone is trying to convince me I’m a soldier.”

“And if it isn’t?”

“Then someone is trying to convince me I’m a criminal.”

Her silence was not agreement. It was attention.

The waitress refilled their cups. A bell over the diner door dinged. The rain thickened, a private applause against the window.

A sedan rolled slowly past the diner. Dark blue. Different make. Same color. Same impossible timing. Or, he thought, there are a lot of blue cars in the world. Same as yesterday.

He watched it disappear.

Lena put her hand over the notebook. “Do not follow that car.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You were calculating whether the driver had seen us.”

“I calculate everything.”

“That’s not the same as knowing.”

The sentence landed and stayed.

Her phone buzzed on the table. She didn’t touch it. His own phone in his pocket seemed to sense the vibration and woke in sympathy: a phantom buzz, the brain inventing muscle memory. He didn’t touch his either.

“What else happened today?” Lena asked.

He exhaled, then told her: at eight sharp the community center newsletter had arrived by email with a verse of the week. It had used a phrase about a person being spared, one he had heard his grandmother say and never worried about until now. Reason one: it was Tuesday; the newsletter always arrived Tuesday. Reason two: whoever wrote the newsletter could also be part of a ring making him think he’d been selected and saved, as if any of this were grace.

At nine he’d received an ad in a music app for a used-car lot running a “Second Chance” sale. The hero image was a blue sedan. Explanation one: his location and last week’s search for affordable vehicles could explain it. Explanation two: someone was stitching the color to him on purpose, a thread he’d pull until it unraveled his day.

At ten he’d seen a post on a neighborhood forum about road closures “down by the warehouses.” The post had two comments: one complaining about police tape, one asking what was happening. Half the city knew before he did. That was not an operation. That was a city being a city. Or it was a city being fed the same story he had been, five minutes ahead.

An hour later a recruiter he had spoken to twice emailed with the subject line: POSITION “ON HOLD.” The body said, We love comeback stories but the client is choosing to pause for stability reasons—let’s circle back in Q4. He stared at the word comeback until it meant nothing. Comm. Back. Why not just say delay. He gave himself the kinder story: everyone delays. The crueler: someone had whispered stability to them in the tone that means safety to a buyer and warning to a seller.

Lena let him stack each item. “You can log each channel,” she said, not lecture so much as suggestion. “Music, advertisements, employment, church, public posts. On their own, every one of those can be random. Together, they can be designed. But we can’t know yet. Your strength will be refusing to prove more than you can.”

“Refusing to,” he said. “That’s a discipline.”

“It’s a choice.”

He gave her the note of the front-door audio file, the one that had arrived the night before, the creak and the swallow of air he couldn’t prove was his. He watched her face carefully for the flicker that would tell him she had already filed him under delusional. She didn’t give him that. She wrote: door. 8/10. Gate? House? Could be theft attempt. Could be stunt. Ask neighbor’s security cam?

“I’ll see if I can find camera angles from the block,” she said. “Not for publishing. For you.”

“That helps.”

Lena looked at her watch, closed the notebook. “I hate to say this part out loud, but—”

“I’m not special,” he said before she could. “I’m one of many.”

“You are specific,” she said. “That’s worse. It means they learned you.”

He wanted to ask who they was again. He didn’t. The word made everything larger than it was and smaller than it could be.

They paid in cash because Lena insisted. He pocketed the receipt. A small ad ran along the bottom: Miracle Motors, Second Chance Event. If you bring this slip, we will double your down. He almost laughed. The word belonged on a devotional, not a sales counter. Or maybe devotionals and sales counters had been speaking the same language his whole life and he had not seen it until now.

Back at the community center, the server room was a closet with a dead plant and a humming rack. He’d started volunteering two months ago when the center’s director, Tasha, had said, “If you want to help, the Wi-Fi dies when it rains.” He had written a grant application at her second-hand desk, replaced two aging access points, and not once had he felt like a genius. Useful, instead. The old calendar thumbtacked to the far wall still read April. He left it. Some mistakes are permission.

His cloud storage chirped on his laptop when he logged in to check whether the front-door audio had a sender he’d missed. A new file sat in a folder named LEGAL—one of the folders he avoided opening unless he had a witness, even if the witness was paper. The file name was too tidy for any document he had filed himself.

MINUTE_ORDER_FAMILY_DIVISION_2020-10-14.pdf.

Created: Today. 10:52 a.m.

Modified: October 14, 2020.

He clicked once, let the preview load in a window. The header showed the seal of a county he knew too well. The body held paragraphs he recognized. It was not the most damning order; it had also not been the end of the world the way it had felt then. His name sat in Times New Roman like a stranger’s. The judge’s credits and disclaimers ended with a sentence: The court has considered the totality of the circumstances. He could not tell whether the italicization in one line had always been there or if this copy performed more. His temples pulsed once, a headache deciding whether to arrive.

He wrote without thinking in his paper notebook.

Confirmed: A file exists. It replicates a document I held. Cloud shows creation today. Two explanations: (1) Fill-in-the-blank sync error after app reinstall; (2) Someone with access inserted it. Unknown: purpose.

He closed the laptop and pressed both palms against the old oak desk until the wood came up through the meat of his hands and pushed back.

At 4:07, Skyler went to his mother’s house.

Maria lived in a narrow brick home near the church where she had volunteered for twenty years. She kept the curtains open and the doors locked. Her refrigerator was covered with drawings, appointment cards, and a grocery list written in three languages because Maria believed multilingual labels made food more interesting. There was a chessboard magnet holding up one of Luna’s school photos—the black pawn in front, the queen behind. A shallow dish on top of the fridge held buttons, foreign coins, and a tiny brass compass beneath a paper label Alexis had lettered in fat, careful marker: TREASURES. On the windowsill, a rain gauge Summer had made from a recycled bottle caught the afternoon’s measure.

Maria answered the door before he knocked.

“You look thin,” she said.

“I ate.”

“Coffee is not eating.”

“Lena says I shouldn’t follow cars.”

“Lena is correct.”

He stepped inside, closed the door, paused the way a person pauses when closing a door has become a sound they listen to afterward. The small radio on the kitchen counter played a hymn from another room. Four notes repeated in the melody. They always had. He had never before thought of notes as a code until now. He hated that too.

Maria looked past him toward the street. “Did anyone follow you?”

He wanted to say yes. The word rose automatically. Yes was an answer that made him feel less foolish. Yes made him the center of a line.

“I don’t know,” he said.

Maria’s face softened the way glass softens under heat; if you blinked you missed the transition. “That is a better answer.”

They sat at the kitchen table. The oilcloth was printed with apples. There was a burn mark at one corner where he had once set a hot pan when he was seventeen and deciding he would never again make a mistake he couldn’t fix.

“You need sleep,” Maria said, pouring tea even though he hadn’t asked.

“People keep saying that.”

“Because you keep needing it.”

He rubbed his jaw where a tightness nested these days. “They put something on my phone.”

“Maybe.”

His head lifted. “Maybe?”

“Maybe someone did,” she said. “Maybe the phone is showing you things because phones show people things. Maybe someone knows how frightened you are and is using ordinary technology to make the fear larger.”

“You make it sound simple.”

“It is not simple. It is only not magic.”

He looked at his mother’s hands. They were small, scarred, and steady. Hands that had changed dressings, signed school forms, paid bills, and once held his face while he cried at nineteen because the startup had rejected his first proposal. She had told him then, You have two eyes and two ears; see and listen before you swing. He had decided instead to become a person who swung fast, because swinging felt noble if you did not look at what you hit.

“Do you think I’m imagining it?” he asked.

“I think you are experiencing something.” Maria placed a teacup on the table in front of him and put the spoon neatly to the side. “I do not know what it means.”

He looked away. The hymn drifted through the door, four notes rising and falling like a question that had been set to music long before he learned to hear questions. His hands found the habit of the cup. The warmth occupied his fingers. Temperature is a fact no one can argue with.

Maria reached for him but stopped before touching his wrist. “May I?” she asked. He nodded. Her hand settled over his. “You are not required to solve the world tonight,” she said.

“I have to solve enough of it to see my girls.”

The room changed after he said that. The argument beneath every sentence stepped into the light.

Maria’s thumb moved once across his knuckles. “Your daughters need a father who is alive, not a father who proves every theory.”

“I’m trying to protect them.”

“Then protect them from this becoming your whole life.”

He swallowed. The sound felt enormous in his head and small in the kitchen.

Maria turned the radio volume down. “Father Matthias was here earlier,” she said. “He is planning the fall fair. He asked if you could help with the livestream again. I told him you were tired.” She smiled, then shook her head at herself. “He said all of us are tired. He said the verse this week is about mercy. It is a good word. Mercy. It is not a code.”

“I saw the newsletter,” he said.

“Then you saw mercy where I saw a reminder to make casseroles for the bereaved.” She held his gaze. “You taught them the four notes last year when the choir could not gather. You turned on the internet and taught old women to press Unmute. That is a thing you did. Remember that.”

Outside, a car passed. Red this time. He made himself breathe until the sound faded. The old clock on the wall ticked. Alexis had once asked whether time was a bird because the clock had a bird painted where a twelve should be. He had told her time was a road. He wondered now if he had been lying by accident.

He stayed until Maria started chopping onions for dinner. He offered to help. She said yes and handed him a knife. The slice and weight of it, the obedience of an onion under a blade, calmed a chamber of his chest. When the cutting board had filled with little translucent pieces, he leaned against the counter and let his shoulder touch his mother’s for a breath. She did not remark on it.

On the way out, he paused to adjust the crooked chessboard magnet on the refrigerator. He put the pawn precisely on the square it had slid off and smiled because it was an insignificant victory and those were the only kind he could win today.

Night folded down like cloth.

He returned to his apartment and placed his keys in the bowl by the door, the learned ritual that says you will be able to find your way out again. He emptied his pockets: the diner receipt; a paperclip bent into a fish by Alexis three summers ago; a parking stub; the card Tasha from the center had printed on her inkjet with the Wi-Fi password in careful block letters. He liked the ordinariness of the pile.

He slept for forty-three minutes.

At 1:26 a.m., he woke to a notification. The phone buzzed against the book on the nightstand twice as if polite, then once with a longer complaint.

NEW FILE ADDED TO CLOUD STORAGE.

The filename was:

2020_JUDGE_SPARED_YOUR_LIFE_FINAL.wav

Fear arrived as heat behind his eyes.

He did not open it.

He placed the phone on the floor, away from the bed, like a cup of poison set on a high shelf. He turned on the lamp. The room answered with light and the old scuff on the wall near the closet took on a mountain’s shape, which would have been funny if he were not alone.

He opened the paper notebook and wrote:

The file exists. I have not listened to it. I do not know who created it. I am afraid of what it may contain.

He paused until the scratch of the pen had forgotten his hand. Then, beneath the entry, he wrote one more sentence.

Fear is not proof, but fear is data about the person carrying it.

He added a box beside the line and checked it. The box was not for fact. It was for a boundary.

His cloud folder on the laptop, asleep on the dresser, made a sound like a distant friend clearing a throat. He looked up in time to see the name of the earlier PDF change in the notifications pane: MINUTE_ORDER_FAMILY_DIVISION_2020-10-14.pdf had become SPARED_YOUR_LIFE_ORDER.pdf. The creation time remained this morning’s. The modification date still read 2020. Two truths on a single line.

He stared at the rectangle of light until it dimmed and then stared at his own words until dawn taught the room a color other than fear.

## Chapter Three
### The First Beat

The first diss track went public at 9:00 a.m. on Wednesday.

By 9:11, it had been reposted forty thousand times.

By 9:30, three news sites were calling it the opening shot of a cyberwar between the United States and China.

By 10:00, Skyler’s name was hidden inside the lyrics.

He watched it happen on a secondhand monitor whose bezel was nicked at the lower right, the kind of imperfection that always drew his eyes. The technology center’s main room smelled like dust and faint solder. Eight teens in mismatched hoodies had chosen the same table and were pretending not to watch two boys solder an LED strip to a controller. A teacher asked if the printer’s jam could be freed by “softly blessing it with cuss words.” The normal hum was oddly loud.

Someone slid into his DMs with a link and an unnecessary flourish of flame emojis. A volunteer posted the same link into the center’s group chat with the caption: “This is… what?” His own message history showed Lena’s text above them all: Do not share this until we talk.

The track was titled RED, WHITE, SILENT. Its cover image showed a white eagle with a cracked beak standing on a circuit board. The caption read: NO FLAG OWNS THE SIGNAL.

Skyler closed the main room door and walked past the rows of donated monitors into the storage room. It was the smallest space in the building, a rectangle between two support pillars, stacked haphazardly with boxes of Ethernet cables and routers with the names of old offices scraped off in ragged squares. The floor had been mopped that morning; something lemony clung to the air.

He recognized the smell. In 2020, when he had more hours than status and more fear than advice, he’d volunteered to mop at church on Saturdays, wiping along baseboards as if he could clean himself out of a system that had not known him before it defined him. On those mornings he had added things to his life that weren’t about fighting: handing out groceries at the shelter; logging into an online class; going to work and going home without checking his phone every time it beeped. At the time it had felt like serving a sentence. Later—this part he had not admitted until this year—he understood that those hours had also made him useful again. He’d learned which cords were load-bearing and which could be cut.

He slid the door shut and let his shoulders drop against it. He put on his headphones, a half-broken pair held together by electrician’s tape and the stubbornness of someone who used to spend more on headphones than on groceries. He pulled the track from Lena’s message and pressed play.

The beat was heavy but not fast. Not aggressive, exactly; confident, measured, like someone walking onto a stage and already receiving applause. A male voice entered beneath a layer of distortion that made it seem like he was speaking through a ventilator.

You built a kingdom out of borrowed names,
sold the keys, then blamed the flames.
Four corners, one room, no windows, no sky—
tell the father why the children ask why.

Skyler paused the track so abruptly the storage room returned too quickly, as if the lights had snapped on in a theater. His pulse made a small misstep.

The line about the children was not impossible. His daughters existed. Anyone who knew his public history could have written it. The “four corners” might be a steal from the logo from Blue Code’s old design files. The song was clever precisely because it didn’t need a secret.

He considered, as he always did now, whether the words were aimed like a weapon or thrown like confetti. The difference mattered less than he wanted it to. Either way, someone could weaponize the confetti after it fell.

He pressed play.

Red side talking, white side lying,
everybody loves a clean-screen dying.
You don’t need a gun when the feed can aim—
give a man a story, make him wear the blame.

The phone vibrated in his hand.

A new message appeared from an account with no profile picture.

YOU BUILT THE DOOR. WE BUILT THE ROOM.

He didn’t take a screenshot. He took a photograph of the screen with a separate camera they used for the kids’ project documentation, a cheap thing with a cracked corner and masking tape over the logo. The photo made him feel like an adult placing a second lock on a door and, by doing so, admitting the first lock might not be real.

He set the phone into a shallow metal drawer beneath the old fax machine and slid the drawer closed. Maybe it was a Faraday cage. Maybe it was hope. He didn’t want to know yet. He pressed play again and let the rest of the track unspool.

When it ended, the silence pressed so quickly that it became sound itself. He realized his shoulders were still against the door. He eased forward.

The storage room door opened as his weight left it.

Caleb stood there holding two coffees and wearing an expression halfway between smirk and worry. His hair had been cut too close on the sides last weekend, making his ears look like they were in a wind tunnel.

“You’re hiding,” Caleb said.

“I’m working,” Skyler said.

“In a closet.”

“Storage room.”

“Right. The famous distinction.”

Caleb extended a coffee and waited for Skyler to accept it. When Skyler didn’t move, he set the cup on a box of “MISC ADAPTERS” written in marker that had bled into the cardboard.

“You hear the song?” Caleb asked. He tried, and failed, to sound like the question had the same weight as “You hear the rain?” or “You hear the printer dying again?”

“Everybody has,” Skyler said.

“It mentions your company.”

“It mentions a symbol my company used.”

“That’s more than most songs mention. I don’t hear many bangers about Cat 6.”

“Who sent you?” Skyler asked.

Caleb’s smile snapped away so cleanly it looked like a trick. “What?”

“Who told you to come in here?”

“Nobody.”

“Did you see the message?”

“What message?”

Skyler regretted all of it immediately. Suspicion moved faster than apology, an old professional reflex that had become a personal curse.

Caleb put his coffee on another box and stepped into the room until the tips of his sneakers touched the mottled edge of the mop’s drying line.

“You think I’m involved?” he asked quietly. “You think I’m one of them?”

“I think someone is using people,” Skyler said, because that was true and also a way not to say yes or no.

“You think I’m one of them,” Caleb repeated. He shook his head, and something in his cheek tightened the way it had when his last partner left.

“I don’t know what I think.”

“That’s not better.”

Caleb exhaled and took half a step back. He looked toward the drawer as if he could sense the phone through metal. “I brought you coffee because you look like death and because you’re my friend. If that makes me a pawn, then apparently the bar for evil has gotten very low.”

Skyler wanted to find a light sentence to knock the heaviness off. What came out was a broken sound that he recognized as a laugh only after he heard it. The sound embarrassed him. Caleb’s eyes softened anyway.

“There,” Caleb said, pointing. “That. You’re still in there.”

“Who?” Skyler said.

“Whoever you were before your phone became a haunted toaster.”

Skyler looked down at the coffee. The lid had a cross-shaped drinking slit that reminded him, unwisely, of church. Of Maria brushing crumbs off tablecloths in the fellowship hall and telling him, in that tone she used when she both loved him and thought he was about to do something foolish, to eat first and fight later.

“Sorry,” he said, meeting Caleb’s eyes.

Caleb nodded but did not accept the apology completely. “Good. Stay sorry for a minute. It’s healthier than being certain.”

They were still standing there when Lena arrived with a printout, which was her version of a siren. She didn’t knock. She was already talking as she stepped inside, the way she did when the sentence mattered more than where she was.

“The song was uploaded from three accounts,” she said.

“Three people?” Skyler asked, allowing himself one second of wanting it to be simple.

“Not necessarily,” Lena said. She handed him the page and then took it back to tap the corner she wanted him to see. “Three accounts using different infrastructure. The timing is too clean.”

“Government?” Caleb asked, too quickly.

“Maybe,” Lena said. “Marketing company. Criminal promotion service. Political group testing an effect. Teenagers with money. We don’t know.”

“Teenagers with money,” Caleb repeated. “Great. Finally a threat we can reason with.”

“Don’t share it,” Lena said to Skyler, as if he hadn’t read her earlier message. She had what his mother called a steady voice—low register, measured length, no rising pitch at the end of a sentence to ask permission. It made people answer her questions. It also made people obey without remembering they’d chosen to obey.

“There’s a second track,” Skyler said.

“I know,” Lena said.

THE EAGLE HAS TWO SHADOWS sat in his inbox like a dare. Its cover showed a red dragon reflected in a cracked American flag, the kind of graphic a million anonymous accounts could argue over while the uploaders counted engagement. The track description accused the first rapper of laundering propaganda through patriotic language, which felt like a copy of a copy of an accusation.

Lena tapped the mousepad and let the second track play through the monitor’s tiny speakers. The voice on the second track was female, calm, almost bored—bored not because she didn’t care, but because she cared and had already seen the next five moves. The beat was sparser, as if the percussion itself had been redacted.

You call it war when the numbers rise,
call it truth when the logo lies.
You point at the ocean, point at the wall,
while the same hand writes both sides of the call.

Skyler closed his eyes for two seconds and opened them again, as if the voice might change when he looked a second time.

He knew the voice.

Not from a person. From a recording that had arrived months ago as a whisper across a secure channel when no one else believed him yet. A woman reading a line from a technical manual about consent architecture, of all unpoetic things. The specific breath between phrases, the way the last consonant softened instead of cut—a metronome for the space a thought takes up when it is being turned into an instruction.

“Ghost,” he said.

Lena stopped the track. Her hand hovered over the touchpad. “Who is Ghost?”

He told her. Not everything and not nothing. He told her about the encrypted files that had started before the phone incident; the four-note sequence that had repeated like a doorbell; the anonymous messages that felt like guidance and warning simultaneously. He did not tell her that he had once opened those messages with a kind of gratitude he had not even felt for himself. He did not say that, for a brief run of weeks, the fact of being seen had been a relief.

“Why didn’t you tell me?” Lena asked.

“Because you would have asked if I was sure,” he said.

“I’m asking now.”

“No.”

“Good.”

Caleb looked from one to the other. “Am I supposed to write that down somewhere? ‘Anonymous mentor named after Halloween decoration may or may not be narrating geopolitical rap battle.’”

Lena didn’t smile. She flicked her eyes toward the drawer. “What else did it send?”

As if the phone had been waiting for its name, the metal drawer buzzed against the wood once. Twice. Three times.

Caleb stared at the drawer the way Skyler had stared at it earlier. A fourth buzz didn’t come.

Skyler opened it. The screen lit his face white-blue.

THE RAP BATTLE IS NOT FOR THE PUBLIC. IT IS FOR YOU.

A location followed.

FOUR CORNERS BAKERY. 8 P.M.

Caleb leaned in to read. “That place has been closed for years,” he said. He didn’t hide the way his voice rose at the end like a question.

“Exactly,” Skyler said, though he couldn’t have explained what exactly meant.

Lena took the phone from him and angled her body so both men would have to go through her to reach it. “We don’t go,” she said.

“Why?” Skyler asked, because the part of him that still wanted to fix things hated not having a lever.

“Because invitations are not evidence,” she said.

“It could be a lead,” he said.

“It could be bait,” she said.

“Those aren’t mutually exclusive,” he said.

“No,” Lena said. “But one of them gets people killed in movies.” She set the phone back in the drawer like she was returning a baby to a bassinet and slid it closed. The soft thud was too parental to comfort him.

He told himself he would follow her lead. He told himself he knew what it meant when the story tried to write him as a protagonist with an appointment in a deserted place.

The afternoon passed under a pressure none of them named. The teens built a small robot that veered left no matter what commands they sent, then cheered for it when it bumped into a chair and pretended to be an escape artist. The printer jam returned and was, eventually, sanctified and healed. The center’s phone rang three times with parents asking if there was still a slot in the free coding class. Skyler answered one of the calls and wrote down a name he recognized as a friend of a friend, and for five seconds the web of ordinary life felt like the realest thing in the room.

At 5:03 p.m., a targeted advertisement appeared on the center’s computer: an eagle-themed cloud security service offering a twenty percent discount if you signed up “before the flag falls.” The ad scrolled over a photograph of a circuit board that looked like the cover of RED, WHITE, SILENT. Skyler closed the window and then documented closing it without knowing why that felt necessary. In the hallway, someone from the church that rented the bottom floor for Sunday school walked by and said, into her phone, “We’re going to talk about how the Father listens even when you don’t know the right words,” and Skyler’s stomach froze and unthawed in one second, which was almost funny, which was not funny at all.

At 6:30, a recruiter emailed his personal account with a subject line that included the words eagle and shadow in the same sentence. He didn’t open it, because he remembered himself.

At 7:40, he stopped remembering.

He left the center after assuring Lena he was going home. It wasn’t a lie yet. He drove three blocks past his apartment and told himself he needed gas and then exited three blocks early and told himself he would turn around at the next light. He circled through an industrial district where the metal roll-up doors made each building look like a mouth with its lips clamped down. He parked in the shadow of an abandoned grocery store with a painted sign that still promised fresh peaches and lottery tickets.

He sat with the engine off and watched the rain draw lines across the windshield too widely spaced to be a curtain and too close to be nothing. Summer used to count raindrops on the back window when she was little, tapping the glass and saying “race you,” as if one drop could want to win. The memory surprised him by not hurting.

He hadn’t gone to Four Corners.

He hadn’t disobeyed Lena.

He had only driven near the address to see whether anyone was watching. It was, he told himself, like checking a smoke alarm to make sure it was beeping about real smoke.

The block where Four Corners Bakery had once existed tried hard to pretend it still did. The green awning remained, letters only half peeled: OUR CORN S. The ghost of a painted baguette floated against the brick, exactly where the stencil had faded quicker than the paint it once hid. When he stood on that sidewalk the year Luna first learned to order things for herself, she’d asked if baguette was the one shaped like a snake or the one shaped like a pillow. “Snake,” he’d said, and they’d laughed at the thought of a bread snake that tasted like butter.

A dark-blue sedan passed the intersection, not fast, not slow, doing that careful speed of someone who had been told there were cameras at either end and who had obeyed without questioning the instruction’s source.

Skyler stayed in the car. He scanned the windows across the street. An older man in a sleeveless shirt smoked in a third-floor window and watched the world the way a person does when the world owes him a story. A light in a second-floor kitchen flicked off and on and off. Somewhere, too far away to place, a bassline pushed and retreated like someone practicing being inconsiderate.

Another vehicle followed. White delivery van. No logo. The streetlight turned the rain around the van into gold pins.

A third car stopped beneath a streetlight two storefronts down from the bakery. The driver was a woman with her hair in a low bun, dark jacket, square shoulders. She opened her door and placed a paper bakery bag on the pavement with the care of someone setting down an animal trap. She did not look around. She didn’t move like a person afraid of being seen. She walked away without haste.

Four Corners.

He didn’t move. He watched his hands on the steering wheel. They looked like someone else’s hands.

He waited five minutes. He counted down his own breath to occupy his head. Inhale for four, hold for two, exhale for six. He did what they had taught him to do when his chest had become a fist in 2020 and he’d been sent outside the courtroom to breathe and to pretend normal air could pass through a body that had become an airtight container. He waited ten.

His phone buzzed.

OPEN THE BAG.

He did not move.

The phone buzzed again.

OR THE NEXT SONG WILL NAME YOUR DAUGHTERS.

His hands became cold in one second, so cold they made his forearms ache. The idea didn’t have to be real to be effective.

He got out of the car. The street felt loud with small sounds: the spread and retract of the rain; the far-off thrum of a train; a dog’s collar ringing like a tiny metallic laugh; the ticking of his own engine cooling.

He crossed toward the bag, hearing each footstep as if it belonged to someone behind him. The sidewalk’s cracks held thin rivers. A cigarette butt floated like a drowned canoe. He stopped beside the bag and didn’t bend immediately. He looked at the bakery door, at the way a line of dust had formed under the seam where the threshold met the door, at the way someone had once tried to wipe the dust and failed.

He crouched. He could feel his jeans dampening at the knee where the leftover rain hadn’t quite abandoned the concrete.

Inside the bag was a paper receipt, a cheap plastic key tagged with a faded 17 in permanent marker, and a square of black fabric embroidered with a red four-corner symbol. The embroidery was tight and professional. The symbol was not his company’s logo, not exactly; it was as if someone had drawn a square and then traced its corners with stitches thick enough to be punctuation.

The receipt contained a list.

11:30 P.M.
WAREHOUSE 17.
BLUE SEDAN.
THREE CASES.

At the bottom, someone had written in block letters with a pen that had started to die mid-stroke:

YOU ALREADY AGREED.

He looked toward the street. All three vehicles were gone. The man in the window had disappeared. The second-floor kitchen light stayed off.

He straightened and the bag crackled, a sound so loud in the quiet block that he flinched. The smell out here was wet asphalt and old yeast. He couldn’t remember if the bakery had been any good.

His phone began to play music. He hadn’t touched it. The sound came through the bag—the phone in his pocket must have connected through cloth. The voice from the first track whispered through the tiny speaker with the hiss of the rainy air turning everything sibilant.

The father is listening.

For one ridiculous second he imagined Luna setting a bishop on the center of the board instead of a knight, her serious face ready to argue that bishops could move like horses if the rules were more reasonable. He thought of Summer pressing her face to the glass and making bargains with raindrops. He thought of Alexis turning a toy compass in a store and saying, so solemnly, “It knows where home is.” He was a man on a sidewalk holding a bakery bag and a key to a door he promised he wouldn’t open.
