He had sat in the third-grade room and read to kids whose parents needed a free hour. He had held a list of addresses and followed it. He had gone to night classes and thought, foolishly and then not, that his brain could be trained to consider some doors not options. That year had been a turning not because someone had allowed it. It had been a turning because he had.

He reached for the pamphlet on the table and turned it over without thinking. On the back, the church had printed a sketch of the mural: four little squares, each the color of a door. Four donation categories. Food. Time. Tutoring. Care. It might have been a coincidence. It was also a map.

“I want to leave my phone with you tonight,” he said to Lena.

She looked up. “Me?”

“You, because you’ll tell me no if you can’t. You, because you won’t answer it for me.”

She took it. “I’ll put it in airplane mode and a drawer,” she said. “If you wake at three in the morning and mistake a pattern for a command, call my landline. It still exists. Its ring will remind you you’re not an app.”

“Do we still have a landline?” Maria asked, perplexed. “With the cord?”

“With a cord,” Lena said, and a small smile made the room briefly less heavy.

The pastor knocked at the open basement door like he needed permission to enter his own building. “The choir kids will be in the sanctuary in ten minutes,” he said. “If you need them quiet, I can ask.”

“We need them to be kids,” Maria said. “Let them be loud.”

A child’s voice floated down almost immediately, counting. One, two, three, four. The notes that followed were not a tune from the radio, not anything with a name. They were four plain tones on the church’s upright piano, soft under a hesitant hand, the same interval played twice and then twice again, a practice piece. Skyler felt his skin lift the way it does when a cold wind hits sweat. Mara’s eyes flicked up, calculating nothing, measuring everything.

“Coincidence,” he said aloud, and he let the word be a friend.

Reyes stood. “I’ll be in touch when we have something besides words,” he said. “For tonight, if someone calls you with a story that completes the sentence in your head, it is an operation. If someone calls you to ask how you are, it is love. Answer the second call.”

“Who calls to ask that?” Skyler asked, half of him cynical, the other half embarrassed by how much he wanted to be wrong.

“Your mother does,” Maria said, simple, the way rivers say we go where we want and then we go where gravity tells us.

They folded the day back into their bodies. Lena tucked the phone in her bag in a pocket he had never noticed before, the way people who have learned about danger become their own storage systems. Mara pocketed the printed logo as if it were a memory she needed to keep outside her head. Reyes carried the folder.

Skyler took one last look at the mural. In the lower corner, he had painted a small compass rose once, an afterthought that became intentional because Alexis always wanted to know which way things were facing. He had made north white against the green and the other points pale blue. It was still there. The paint had cracked into a pattern that looked like age lines on a palm.

On their way out, the air lifted. Summer rain had moved in while they had argued, the sky getting its say with a gentle insistence. It tapped the awning and the pastor’s jacket and Maria’s hair. Summer rain. Of course.

Skyler paused at the side door, where the box had sat. He had thought they would scrub the step and it would look like nothing had happened. It looked like a step.

“Tomorrow,” he said to no one in particular, or to the doors, “we mark the wall. Confirmed. Possible. Unknown.”

“Good,” Lena said from behind him, her voice snugged into the rain.

“And we take a picture of the wall,” Mara added. “Because someone will try to make it say something it doesn’t. That includes you.”

“Noted,” he said.

The government sedan returned at the end of the block and paused again, engine idling. It could have been a coincidence. It could have been a neighbor waiting to turn. It could have been a man named Daniel checking his mirrors.

Skyler didn’t let his attention inhabit any of those houses. He tucked his hands into his jacket because he had learned not every motion needed to announce fear. He met Maria under the umbrella she had finally opened, their shoulders touching the way they had when he was small and she had told him to look at the sky during storms so he would not count the seconds between lightning and thunder and mistake the counting for control.

In the doorway behind him, the pastor had taped a fresh sheet of paper to the corkboard. A notice about a food drive. The clip art was a house with four square windows. Someone had shaded one of the windows a little too dark. It would have bothered him before. Tonight, he looked at the crooked, earnest rectangles and saw the hours they meant. He saw work that did not ask for his explanation to be allowed.

As they stepped into the rain, his phone quiet in someone else’s bag, his voice still in his throat, Skyler understood a thing he had not allowed himself to understand: it was not that the world would stop trying to choose for him when he did everything right. It was that some choices he could still make would outlast the story.

Behind them, from the basement, the child’s four notes became five. A missed key, a giggle, another try. A door that hadn’t been there one hour ago opening an inch, not because someone had broken a lock but because a hand had learned where to push.

## Chapter Ten
### The Convenience Store

The convenience store was called Sunrise, although it had no windows on the east side and never opened before six.

At 2:11 a.m., Skyler received the stupidest financial offer of his life.

That was saying something.

The message claimed to come from a broker attached to the **Jade Harbor syndicate**, a fictional transnational criminal group in the novel.

**SEND $800. RECEIVE $1,000,000 LIQUID.**

Skyler read it once.

Then again.

A million dollars for eight hundred was not finance.

It was either a scam, laundering bait, counterfeit money, or somebody testing whether desperation had finally made arithmetic optional.

The message included photographs of bundled cash, a crypto wallet balance, and a screenshot from a video by **Mason Blaze**, the fictional world’s biggest giveaway creator. In the clip, Blaze stood in a warehouse surrounded by pallets of novelty money while text promised a million-dollar challenge.

The video was real.

The connection was not.

Someone had cut pieces of a public stunt into a private financial trap.

Skyler’s bank account had less than rent in spendable dollars.

On paper, strangers believed he had an inheritance from the richest man alive.

In crypto screenshots, he looked rich.

In actual U.S. dollars, he was nearly broke.

That contradiction made him perfect prey.

He did not send the eight hundred.

Then a second message appeared.

**YOU DON'T HAVE TO PAY. WE ALREADY CREDITED YOU.**

A wallet he did not recognize showed a balance of one million.

The next morning, a package of money appeared in a vehicle connected to one of the drops.

Some bills were real.

Some were novelty notes printed for promotion.

Some were high-quality counterfeits.

And some were not dollars at all, only QR-coded certificates pointing toward crypto nobody could redeem.

That mixture was worse than a box of obvious fakes.

It created a story in which Skyler could be made to look rich, broke, fraudulent, bribed, or stupid depending on which photograph somebody published.

Within forty-eight hours, investigators were asking whether he had knowingly accepted counterfeit funds.

The question climbed high enough that the fictional **President Rowan Voss** was briefed—not because Skyler was important, but because the same counterfeit-and-crypto pipeline touched a broader corruption case.

Voss ordered an independent hold on punitive action until the provenance of the money could be established.

To Skyler, it felt like the president had saved his life.

To Reyes, it was paperwork doing what paperwork was supposed to do.

“Do not make a mythology out of due process,” Reyes told him.

Skyler almost laughed.

“Can I be grateful for it?”

“Yes.”

“Can I be dramatic about it?”

“No.”

At 2:18 a.m. the next night, Skyler went to Sunrise because the tap in his kitchen tasted like pennies and because the darkness in his apartment had thickened into a practical kind of threat. Insomnia had converted the rooms into an interrogation space. Every object seemed arranged to ask the same question: what did you miss?

He walked there in a hooded sweatshirt and a jacket he had not remembered owning until he found a crumpled coin receipt in its pocket—a purchase from a hardware store whose address he didn’t recognize. He put the receipt in a plastic bag because that was what he did now, even with meaningless slips. He had learned to store the dumbest details because later, sometimes, dumbness turned into proof.

The Sunrise’s parking lot was a damp square of light in a city that had folded in on itself for the night. The store’s sign stuttered in pink and gold and then stabilized. The hum of the coolers ran like low weather. Inside: the smell of burnt coffee, sugar, lemon cleaner, and the faint wet of someone’s umbrella. A slot machine near the window glowed in a patient, blue heartbeat.

The clerk at the counter was young, maybe twenty, with a silver hoop in his left ear and a second earring shaped like an arrow in the right. He looked at Skyler’s face, then flicked his eyes to the phone in Skyler’s hand, then away, and the movement was so quick that Skyler felt it land on his skin.

He noticed now. He noticed everything. The noticing was not a gift. It was a condition.

The floor tiles formed a grid of pale squares, scuffed in a pattern he recognized from chess books—Luna’s favorite endgame studies had taught him that a pawn could become a queen if it survived all the way across. Four aisles. Freezer doors along the back. A stockroom door hung slightly ajar, a slip of light knifing outward. He counted exits without meaning to: front door, side door, stockroom, and a curious notch in the back wall near the air filters that looked like an emergency release. He counted the ceiling vents. He listened for cameras. One above the lottery display rotated with an anxious tick.

He took a bottle of water from the second cooler, felt the condensation bead against his palm and run stuttered lines over his knuckles.

The music changed.

It had been a cheerful synth pad before, a woman singing the kind of phrases people paste on magnets—bright little lies that sound like hope from a distance. He couldn’t catch the exact words now; he snapped his head toward the ceiling speaker and it was already fading.

The next track crossfaded in before the first had ended. A deeper voice entered over a simple drumbeat. Four hard hits and then the bass. The voice was male, warm, confiding, the kind of voice a person thinks belongs to a friend.

He still thought, sometimes, that if he could catalog everything in order, his mind would stop reading it as a message.

He stayed where he was, one hand on the cooler door, palm wet, his reflection in the glass looking like a person kept behind it.

The first couplet landed with a confidence that felt like a hand on the back of his neck.

He said the morning was quiet, but the morning had a name.
He said the phone was empty, but the phone remembered the flame.

His stomach dropped with that irrational lurch he got on dark highways when a deer’s reflection flashed. His mind went white for a second and then came back crowded.

The clerk looked at him, eyes flicking to the speaker and back. He chewed on a straw whose end had been flattened into a ribbon.

“Is this your playlist?” Skyler heard himself ask. His voice came out like someone else doing an impression of calm.

The clerk shrugged, mouth twisting. “It’s automatic,” he said. He gestured toward the tiny terminal behind the counter where the store’s management app lived. “It does it by itself. Barometric pressure. Moon cycles. I don’t know.”

“That’s not funny,” Skyler said. He meant the joke or he meant the fact that the music had used his word—morning—not dawn, not daybreak. He wasn’t sure.

The clerk’s hand stilled. He was young enough not to know how many kinds of not-funny there were.

“Who controls it?” Skyler asked.

“I don’t know.” The clerk looked genuinely at a loss, then added, “Corporate.” He said the word like it could absorb blame.

The song shifted. The melody edged upward; the snare doubled.

Four corners in a paper bag, blue metal by the rail,
every man who thinks he’s free has already left a trail.

Blue metal by the rail. He did not keep a gun. And still the line slid a memory up into his mouth. The summer before, the coin receipt in his pocket, the rail at the river where he had watched a train drag its cargo past like a slow threat. Four corners in a paper bag. A takeout order? Or the folded receipt the stranger had given him by the laundromat last week: a square of 3-by-3 thermal paper, the vehicle description, the warehouse. He had reported it. He had documented it. He had refused to go to the drop. He had taken the right action. And now a song inside a convenience store in the night was making it feel like his refusal had been observed by a machine with taste.

He put the water on the counter and didn’t take his palm off the plastic. It steadied him to feel an object that didn’t talk back.

The clerk’s eyes widened, then did that nervous calculation young men do when they have to decide whether to get involved in trouble before it arrives.

Outside, a car passed with its stereo loud enough to buzz the store windows. The same beat. Not exactly—that was impossible to know—but the same stagger of bass in the chest and the same subject matter, because the voice outside was rapping about “drops” and “honesty” and “fathers who feed the beast.”

He turned toward the window. The parking lot’s dark was a thin skin over a pool of reflected light. Tires hissed on wet pavement; the rain had come in the hour he had lost to staring at the ceiling. The car’s taillights arced red and were gone.

His temporary phone buzzed in his hand. He had set it to not show previews, but the notification slid down anyway as if it had been given permission somewhere he couldn’t find: a suggested video from a channel he had never subscribed to.

THE DROP THAT NEVER HAPPENED.

The thumbnail was an aerial still of a warehouse district and a car whose make and color were identical to the one in the paper receipt. It wasn’t necessarily the same photograph he had seen in the news—aerial shots of roofs could look like any other roof—but his heart didn’t want nuance right now.

He did nothing. Doing nothing was a skill he had not possessed six months earlier. He had taught it to himself by force. He felt his thumb want to open the video. He kept it still and breathed into his chest like Hanna had once taught him—two slow counts in, a longer one out.

The clerk whispered, “They’re everywhere.”

Skyler turned his head just enough to catch the clerk’s mouth forming the words.

“Who?” he asked.

The clerk’s mouth closed. His eyes went wary. “I didn’t say anything,” he said, which was what people said when they wanted to pull a word back into their throat and couldn’t.

The phone buzzed again.

YOU FINALLY NOTICED.

Letters hard as blocks. No sender name, just the blank gray square that looks like privacy and is sometimes only a missing field. The message slid out of the notifications and into the standard screen, as if the device had been unlocked for it.

In the same second, a second message arrived, stacked beneath it with cheerful impatience.

DON’T TRUST THE PEOPLE HELPING YOU.

He had learned to screen-record everything important. He swiped down, tapped the record circle, and watched the red timer count its seconds in the top corner. That stupid red dot had become a kind of medicine. He held the phone more tightly than he needed to and then forced his grip to loosen. He put both messages in the frame and inhaled to slow the tremor in his wrist. The messages did not delete themselves. They did not transpose. They sat like facts might sit: real because they were present, unproven because they could have been sent by anyone.

Rain scratched at the window. The door opened on a bell that tried to be charming and only succeeded in sounding like glass touching.

A man stepped in wearing a gray coat that had once been expensive and now had a slick of rain along each seam. His hair was wet, his face pale from the weather. A red cap.

Skyler felt his throat close around a name that wasn’t a name so much as a category. Buyer.

The buyer from 2020.

The cap was the same flat shade, the same stiff brim, the same way the logo hung low across the man’s brow. He could have been anyone. Six years is a long time to carry a stranger’s hat in your head. His memory was not a camera. He knew that. He knew the first thing fear does is supply a costume. He knew that grief will tell any face to put on the one it needs to believe it has found.

The man did not look at him. He moved like someone with cold hands. He walked to the freezer, opened the door with his wrist like a person who didn’t want to leave fingerprints for personal reasons that had nothing to do with crime, and stared at the rows of ice like men who won’t let themselves buy the brand they want.

In the space between the freezer door and the sleeve of the gray coat, when the man reached inside, a tattoo slid into view on the pale inside of his wrist: a small four-corner mark, not elaborate, just a square split by a hairline crack that made two equal halves that didn’t match. It could have been a brand logo. It could have been a personal symbol. It could have been a drunk idea someone took to a strip-mall parlor at three in the morning in 2018. But it was his symbol—it looked like the cracked mirror that had flickered on the backdrop of the rap tracks, a black line through reflected error. It was the same geometry that a stranger had worn on a hoodie in a clip that Lena had sent: a mundane shape rebranded as private portent.

Skyler set the bottle a little too hard on the counter and the water inside slapped the plastic with an unhelpful sound.

He ran.

His body decided ahead of him. His mind scrambled behind.

The bell above the door nicked at his skin as he blew through into rain that was denser than it had looked from inside. The parking lot tilted; the blacktop steamed; the neon from the sign painted itself on pools that did not want to hold still.

He forgot to check for traffic. A truck groaned past like a threat from someone who doesn’t want to hurt you but can’t understand why you stepped where wheels would be.

A hand caught his coat from behind. The hand did not wrench, did not yank. It closed and then withdrew when he spun.

Caleb stood where the hand had been.

“Don’t—” Skyler began, and then had to pick a word. “—touch me.”

Caleb’s eyes were bloodshot and apologetic, which was his grinless version of concern. He lifted both hands like a person in a hostage video. “Copy.”

“Why are you here?” Skyler asked.

“Because you called me twelve times.” Caleb said it with a careful evenness that made it a question without asking it.

Skyler looked at his phone. The lock screen was a field of wet reflections; his finger made the face appear. The call history did not show any calls made in the last hour. His thumb scrolled without orders from the rest of him. Nothing.

Caleb’s expression tried and failed to stay unamused. “I’m not following you,” he said.

“I didn’t say you were,” Skyler said.

“You didn’t have to.”

The store door opened behind them and the bell returned, the tiny glassy noise suddenly foreign in the rain. The man in the red cap stepped outside, plastic bag in one hand, a carton of ice cream boxed and sweating through the other.

He looked directly at Skyler. The looking was simple and human and had no theatricality in it. Then he smiled. Not a smirk. A courtesy. Like two people caught in the same unpleasant weather acknowledging one another’s bodies.

Skyler moved. That was what his body wanted to do: insert itself into whatever was happening and find a cause at a distance it could touch. His weight went forward.

Caleb stepped into the path of that motion and put a hand—not hard, just there—on Skyler’s chest.

“Don’t,” he said.

“I know him,” Skyler said.

“No, you don’t,” Caleb said. “You know a hat you remember.”

The man in the red cap turned and walked toward the bus stop. His steps said nothing. The red of the cap was dull, the coat dragged water off the cuffs. He did not run. He did not look back. He had purchased a pint of ice cream at two in the morning in a gray coat and he was going to take that ice cream somewhere and eat it, and that truth was too simple to hold.

Skyler took a full step, then another. The space between him and the bus stop seemed designed to be the kind of space a person could cross if he was willing to lose the rest of his life to it.

His phone buzzed with the same impatience as the second message had earlier. He made himself look because not looking was how people became evidence to their own bad stories.

A message from Mara:

THAT’S EXACTLY WHAT THEY WANT.

Two warring feelings arrived together: heat along his jaw that said don’t be controlled, and something more patient under it that said recognize the template. Don’t be the person they’re writing.

He stopped. It was like slamming brakes on a boat. He rocked forward, then back, felt himself steady. He had felt this before, during the long months after the hearing in 2020, when he had made a decision to be useful instead of being only injured. He had signed up to stack boxes at the food pantry that the church ran; he had taken an evening class in statistics at the community college because it made his mind work in ways his fear couldn’t follow. He had spent Saturday mornings tutoring kids whose parents worked two jobs. He had left his phone in a drawer on Sundays and gone to sit in a pew near his mother and touch the varnished wood when the hymns were too much. At the time he had called it probation in his head—punishment turned into discipline—but months later he had understood that it had been a gift that had arrived in a shape he didn’t recognize, an unexpected opening to become useful again.

The red-capped man boarded the bus. He grabbed the pole and stood because there were already a dozen night people inside. On the side panel, a grainy advertisement promised a low-interest something that could be read as “trust” if you wanted that word. A woman with a silver grocery tote in her lap looked at him and then away. The doors sighed closed. The bus pulled into the wet and then was part of it.

Skyler’s whole body hurt with the desire to run after it. His vision reached and snagged. The bus’s taillights were two coins dropped in the rain.

Caleb read his face and didn’t say anything for a moment. Then, because he was Caleb, he said, “If we go, it’s because we choose to go. Not because a bus tells you who you are.”

It should have felt patronizing. It didn’t. It felt like resistance.

The song inside the store changed again. It moved through tones like rooms. This time there were no words to tell him how to hear it. Only four notes, flat and spread, the kind of motif a commercial jingle would use because it could fit anywhere. Four notes with space between the second and third that made the third sound like a door if you were the kind of person who had doors on your mind.

He listened because not listening would have been worse.

The rain came harder, and then softer again, the way rain never asks for permission. A delivery truck idled by the curb, coughing diesel. Somewhere on the block, a distant porch light clicked on and a dog thought about what it was for and then decided to bark.

Skyler became aware of his hand on his phone like it had been gripped to a rail. He unhooked it from himself and, because habit had become a kind of ethics, he opened the Truth Protocol app and recorded what had happened in language he could live with.

[Truth Protocol: Field Entry—Aug 18, 2:28 a.m.]

Observed: In-store playlist changed to track with lyrics referencing “morning” and “phone remembered the flame.” No direct sender identified. Clerk stated playlist is automatic. Second verse referenced “four corners” and “trail.”

Observed: Passing car played track with similar beat/subject (“drops,” “fathers”). Cannot verify same track.

Observed: Temporary device recommended video titled “THE DROP THAT NEVER HAPPENED” with thumbnail resembling prior news image (warehouse district). Did not click. Recording started at 2:20 a.m. (attached).

Observed: Two messages received from unknown number(s): “YOU FINALLY NOTICED.” “DON’T TRUST THE PEOPLE HELPING YOU.” Screen-recorded receipt of both (attached). Sender identity unknown. Delivery method unknown. Could be spoofed.

Observed: Male, approx. late thirties to mid-forties, gray coat, red cap, wet hair, purchased ice cream. Wrist tattoo visible when reaching into freezer: small square with hairline crack (resembles cracked-mirror imagery seen in prior Choir asset covers). Eye contact + smile. Boarded Route 11 bus, 2:23 a.m.

Interpretation: Two plausible explanations—
1) Coordinated psychological operation exploiting commercial playlist scheduling, passing traffic, and content suggestions to create perceived narrative targeting.
2) Heightened pattern recognition causing selection of coincidental stimuli as meaningful.

Action: Did not pursue. Do not escalate. Note four-note motif in current in-store track. Record tone sequence if possible.

He hit stop. He felt stupid transcribing rain and verses into a field report like he was an intern at a surveillance agency he had not applied to join. He also felt his breath even out in his ribs. He made himself save it instead of rewriting it to make himself braver or less. Evidence meant the thing that happened stayed crude.

A voice came on the in-store speaker between the four notes—an ad, the kind that always tries to smile at you with its vowels. “New this week—Door Four Energy Drink. Open the last door. Taste the sky.” He wasn’t sure if he had heard “door” or if his skull had made it, but the cadence made the back of his neck prick. The clerk rolled his eyes in a human gesture that had nothing to do with Skyler and probably everything to do with hearing the same six ads on loop.

“Hey,” the clerk said to Skyler, like he had chosen to risk involvement after all. “You okay, man?”

“Yeah,” Skyler lied.

The clerk looked past him toward the rain and then back at his face like he was trying to calculate kindness against policy. “If it helps,” he said, “the playlist does weird stuff at night. It’s not about you. It’s about, like, analytics? My cousin told me he saw a cat video in the laundromat that used the weather to—” He stopped, palms up, grin tugging at the corner of his lip. “I’m saying it’s nobody.”

“Or it’s everybody,” Caleb said dryly.

“What?” the clerk asked.

“Nothing,” Caleb said. “We’re leaving.”

They moved toward the door. Skyler took two steps and then saw a little display by the register he hadn’t registered before: a line of keychains on a wire spindle, enamel shapes for impulse buying. One was a chess king; one was a raindrop with a smiley face. One was a tiny compass charm, cheap metal with a needle that barely moved, enough like the one tied to Alexis in his memory that he couldn’t help the hitch in his chest.

He closed his eyes for the short space of that hurt and let it hold him still. He thought of Summer’s hair after a downpour, how she would tilt her head to listen to rain drum the plastic of the recycling bin; he thought of Luna leaning over a board, her thumb flat against a pawn to steady it, the way she grinned without teeth when a trap sprung; he thought of Alexis turning a compass in her palm and naming imaginary destinations as if she had already traveled there. Remember your children as people, not symbols, he told himself, and then had to smile a little at the idea that he now coached himself into not turning love into evidence.

“Hey,” Caleb said quietly, following his line of sight. “You want the compass?”

“No,” Skyler said. “I want to go home and not treat a bus like a god.”

They stepped back into the weather. The rain had changed its mind about being heavy; it hung like a fine net now. The street lamps held out their hands and caught it in cones.

A sedan slowed at the intersection and an ad on its roof glowed and then shifted, the new image not a message to him but also structurally designed to look like a message to anybody: a smiling woman holding a parcel with the caption YOUR DROP, ON TIME. He could feel his mind trying to take it personally like a muscle flexing from habit. He made himself turn his face a little toward the sky instead. The night was a lid, but it had stars through the lid if you were rude enough to insist they were there.

Caleb bumped his shoulder, offering contact and also a nudge. “Coffee?” he said.

“Water,” Skyler said, remembering the bottle he had paid for and left on the counter. He went back inside to retrieve it. The clerk handed it to him with a tiny nod that had more in it than transactions require. A small wooden cross hung on a chain at the clerk’s neck; Skyler hadn’t seen it before. It made him think of his mother’s voice on those Sundays when the news had made him raw and she had said, gently, “Armor is not a wall, mijo. It’s a choice.” He had decided then that he could help without believing he would be sainted for it. He had filled crates with canned pineapple and evaporated milk. He had run cables under tables at a senior center for a vaccine signup event and he had not photographed himself while doing it. Use is a verb, he reminded himself. Not a trophy.

Outside again, the city felt like it had shifted its weight. The bus was gone. The soaked red cap was a story he would have to not tell.

Under the light of the awning, a kid on a BMX bike circled twice before hunching over his handlebars to read his phone. He wore a black hoodie with a white emblem over the breast: a little square with a crack through it, the split done like a lightning bolt you buy. It was the same cracked mirror motif. Or it was a skate brand, one of those small labels whose designers all share fonts and shapes. His stomach tilted. He could feel his mind readying to interpret it as proof. The kid lifted his eyes and, because he must have felt a man’s attention on him, looked up. The look was pure animal glance: who’s in my space? It had nothing coded in it.

Skyler swallowed. He had the adrenaline taste in his mouth, bright and metallic as the tap water that had sent him here. He lifted his phone and made a show of checking it without pointing the lens at the kid. The four notes inside the store reached him again through the thin door glass. He pressed the microphone icon in the Truth Protocol app and hummed the four tones under his breath into the recording, embarrassed by his own voice and doing it anyway.

“Four notes, equal spacing, whole step between one and two, bigger interval to three, then drop,” he said softly into the mic. “Possible motif linked to Choir assets. Or elevator music with ambitions.” He stopped the record and tagged it with a little blue shell emoji he had set up in the app for anything that felt like the sea’s monotonous intelligence: always there, always the same, always changing.

“What?” Caleb asked.

“Nothing,” Skyler said.

“It’s never nothing,” Caleb said, and it was not accusatory. It was weary.

The kid on the bike pedaled away, his chain whispering, his hood dark like a second night inside the night. A couple hurried under a single umbrella, moving like a small organism designed for weather.

Skyler’s phone chimed again. An employment board notification this time, automatic and bland: We noticed you have experience with identity systems! New contract: marketing drop for a heritage brand, 20 hrs/wk, remote. DROP capitalized, the word an industry term any ad agency would use. He exhaled through his nose, half laugh, half surrender to the ridiculous density of coincidence that real life can achieve if it tries, or if your mind is tuned too tight.

He did not click that either.

Caleb looked at the message flashed in the ambient glow and raised an eyebrow like a man trying to win a game without pieces. “It’s a word people use,” he said.

“I know.” Skyler put the phone back in his jacket pocket. “I know.”

They walked toward the corner where the awning ended. The rain misted their faces. A church bulletin lay half-soaked on the curb, the ink bleeding into itself. He stopped long enough to read the top line because he couldn’t not. It said: Sunrise Community—Food Pantry Volunteers Needed. A Bible verse sat under it in italics he could not fully make out because the water had done its work, something about mourning and joy. He did not take the paper. He did not need another artifact.

He looked back once through the glass at the clerk, who gave him a short wave like a person choosing to ally with decency in small ways. He looked through the glass again at his own reflection layered under the store’s lights, hair damp, eyes older than they had been at the beginning of the month. In that reflection he could see the cracked-mirror emblem on the revolving display of sunglasses by the register—a brand stamp, not a prophecy. He could also see how easy it would be to live like a person a mirror spoke to.

The four notes ended. The next in-store track began with a chirpy ad about tires. The world continued on its cheap rhythms.

He stood under the awning until his heart stopped doing its bird impression. He thought of Luna’s careful hand on a pawn. He thought of Summer’s rain. He thought of Alexis’s compass, the needle trembling and then settling. He would tell them one day—not the fear, not the every-detail chaos—but the part where he had stopped and chose not to be directed by a bus, a hat, a jingle. He would tell them about how to make a door where you are told there is a wall.

“Let’s go,” he said.

They crossed the lot. The blacktop held their reflections like shy friends. A car turned in, then changed its mind and went on. A side street vomited steam.

Behind them, the convenience store hummed. The rotating camera ticked. Somewhere in the space between the speaker and the playlist—between whatever “automatic” meant and how badly a man can want his pain to be a language—those four notes repeated once more, a short echo that could have belonged to the song, or could have been a second speaker catching up, or could have been an artifact of his own attention, trained now to hear the pattern before it exists.

He didn’t go back to check.

He put his head up. He felt the rain. He walked.

At the corner, a man came up out of the apartment stoop shadow, wearing a jacket with a stitched emblem on the sleeve: a small square with a hairline crack. The kind of logo that means nothing to anyone until it means everything to you. The man lit a cigarette, cupped the flame against the wet, and never looked at them. The smoke was the ordinary kind that makes a cold night smell like habit.

Skyler’s breath hitched once, a pulse, and then returned to its work.

He did not chase a bus. He did not interrupt a stranger’s ice cream. He pressed his phone into his palm long enough to feel the shape of a tool that can be asked to carry truth, not dictate it.

Behind him, the four notes trembled and disappeared into the thin rain. Ahead, the street split into four ways like the simple problem of a map. He picked one because it led home.

## Chapter Eleven
### Seven Jobs

By the time the seventh job disappeared, Skyler had stopped asking what one more loss could take from him.

He already knew the inventory.

A house.

Four cars, each loss carrying its own humiliating logistics—keys surrendered, payments missed, repairs abandoned, rides begged, the ordinary machinery of independence stripped down one vehicle at a time.

The daily shape of his family.

His fiancée.

Years with his children that could not be rescheduled.

Seven jobs.

Eight girlfriends or almost-relationships, seven of them ending inside the same fog of fear, interference, mistrust, bad timing, and his own increasingly damaged ability to tell one from another.

The pattern was cruel because it did not have to be perfect.

The first car went when money tightened.

The second became collateral damage in a season when work vanished faster than bills.

The third died mechanically while his attention was somewhere else.

The fourth disappeared from his life after another financial collapse.

None of those losses alone proved sabotage.

Together, inside Skyler's exhausted mind, they became four empty parking spaces in a life that used to move.

The house went the same way—not in one cinematic seizure, but through paperwork, money, separation, and the slow realization that a key can stop belonging to you before the metal leaves your hand.

Then came the relationships.

A suspicious message arrived before a date.

A private detail appeared somewhere it should not.

A woman received something anonymous.

Skyler panicked.

She asked whether he trusted her.

He asked questions that sounded like accusations.

The relationship fractured.

Again.

Again.

Again.

By the eighth, he could no longer tell whether hackers were breaking up his relationships or whether the people behind the pressure had simply taught him a style of fear capable of finishing the job for them.

That possibility was worse.

You did not need to control a man forever if you could teach him to destroy whatever he tried to protect.

The first job ended with an email that arrived at 7:14 a.m., before he had poured milk into his coffee. The subject line read: Alignment Pause. The body, written by no one and everyone, used words that had been sanded to a glass. Thank you for your contribution. We remain impressed by your skills. Due to a routine review of our current resourcing, we’re pausing work across several priorities. We’ll be in touch as things stabilize.

The words routine review were a kind of joke the world told him to his face.

He raised the phone closer to his eyes, as if the pixels could blink and apologize. A banner ad slid into place beneath the message, animating a cartoon rocket and the text: New Career? Seven Steps to Start Fresh. He could have laughed if his hands had not been shaking from a night of half-sleep.

His inbox refreshed. The email dropped lower as replies to replies churned. He took a screenshot, then remembered that he no longer trusted screenshots and used the temporary device’s screen recorder instead. The little red dot pulsed. He felt watched by his own record of being watched.

He stood at the counter while the coffee went cold. Outside, the street still had that blue smell morning carries when heat has not yet claimed the day. A delivery truck idled too long. Somewhere a radio played a song about getting back up. He did not believe the song was about him. He wrote in the notebook anyway:

Routine review. First job ended by email. Confirmed: message timestamp, sender. Unknown: motive, whether selection was targeted.

He tore a clean square from the printer and wrote the date in heavy ink. He taped it to the wall above his desk. One rectangle of white against beige.

The second job ended with a phone call in which no one used the word fired.

“We’re entering a pruning season,” the woman in HR said. He had not spoken to her before that minute. Her voice was the same tone he had heard two days earlier in a church livestream when Pastor Miguel at Covenant North had said sometimes God pruned us for the sake of future fruit.

Skyler stared at the plant on his own windowsill. It was dying because he kept forgetting to water it.

“A pruning season,” he repeated, testing it for code.

“It’s not personal,” she said. “We loved your ideas for access controls. You’re attentive. It’s just—our partners have concerns.”

“What partners? Concerns about what?”

“We’re not at liberty to disclose vendor names. Legal has advised me to say routine review. We can offer a half retainer for two weeks.”

He inhaled. He had learned to hear the edges of words rather than their centers.

“What did you hear about me?”

“I’m very sorry this is happening,” she said, in the way someone might read a condolence note sent by a stranger.

“Is it because of the Protocol?” he asked.

“The… protocol?”

“My documentation. My record of events.”

Silence, a page turn, the faint click of keys. Somewhere on her end an air-conditioner started humming. On his, a crow landed on the fence and yelled at nobody.

“We’re going to wish you the very best,” she said.

“You didn’t answer my question.”

“I’m sorry—I have to get to another call.”

The line filled with a soft ringtone that used four notes, the last one held just a second longer than it needed. He listened to the repetition until the system cut him off.

On the wall, he added the second rectangle beside the first.

The third job was the one Skyler remembered too clearly.

It began before the Glitch had fully taught him to distrust attention.

The fictional company was called **Northstar Components**, a manufacturing-and-logistics operation where machines broke for understandable reasons and production numbers either cleared the screen or did not.

Skyler liked that.

Physical work had honesty.

A part could be defective.

A line could jam.

A schedule could be wrong.

You fixed what you could prove was broken.

His direct supervisor, Ben, was practical, tired, and fair.

Ben's boss was **Vivian Cross**.

Vivian ran operations across several facilities. She was smart enough to understand a problem before most people finished explaining it and charismatic enough to make a room adjust when she entered.

At first she treated Skyler like a promising employee.

Then like a favorite.

Then like something neither of them was willing to name inside a workplace.

She stopped at his area more than her job required.

She remembered his school schedule.

She asked about the girls.

When he fixed a production bottleneck that had been costing the floor half an hour each shift, she arrived the next morning with his coffee exactly right.

“You stalk my order history?” he asked.

“Maybe I pay attention,” she said.

The line stayed with him.

At the time, his life with Kaylee was already breaking in ways that did not fit neatly on a calendar. Love had not disappeared. The structure around it was failing.

Vivian knew enough to understand there was pain.

She did not ask enough questions to understand the whole thing.

That made her attention easier.

After the engagement ended, Skyler reacted with the emotional judgment of a man determined to prove that heartbreak had not made him undesirable.

That was how the two dancers entered the story.

They were two women he met who happened to work as exotic dancers, were extremely attractive, knew exactly how absurd the situation looked, and found Skyler's combination of software obsession and wounded confidence entertaining.

He thought dating both of them sounded funny.

Caleb called it “a hostile penetration test against your emotional infrastructure.”

Skyler told him to shut up.

Caleb said, “Critical vulnerability confirmed.”

For a few weeks, Skyler laughed again.

He went out.

He made bad decisions that were not crimes.

He posted more of his life online than a cybersecurity-minded person should ever admit.

Then a photograph appeared.

Skyler in the middle.

One dancer leaning against his shoulder.

The other laughing into the camera.

Nothing illegal.

Nothing connected to Northstar.

Nothing Vivian needed to see.

Which meant, naturally, that she saw it.

At 6:03 the next morning she messaged him.

**We need to revisit your judgment.**

At 6:07:

**Leadership carries visibility.**

At 6:11:

**See me before shift.**

Her office had glass walls and blinds designed to turn transparency into privacy in one pull.

She closed them.

Skyler sat.

Vivian remained standing.

“So,” she said.

“So?”

“Busy weekend?”

He knew.

He also knew enough not to say he knew.

“It was fine.”

“Fine.”

She moved a folder half an inch across her desk.

“You know people talk.”

“About my work?”

“About people.”

“That is not really an answer.”

“No,” she said. “It isn't.”

The executive disappeared for a second.

What remained was a woman trying very hard not to look hurt.

“You make bad choices when you're hurt,” she said.

Skyler stared at her.

“You don't know enough about my life to say that.”

“I know more than you think.”

The sentence landed wrong.

“What exactly are we meeting about?”

Vivian looked toward the closed blinds.

“Your future here.”

“Because I went out with two women?”

Her eyes sharpened.

“I didn't say that.”

“You didn't have to.”

Silence held.

Then Vivian said the one sentence that made pretending impossible.

“I shouldn't care who you go home with.”

Skyler waited.

She looked away.

“But I do.”

For half a second, he felt flattered.

Then he felt trapped.

That was the tragedy of it: Vivian might genuinely have cared about him. Care itself was not the problem. Power was.

A feeling held by an equal can become a conversation.

A feeling held by the person above your boss can become weather.

“Vivian—”

“Don't.” Her hand came up. “I'm not asking you for anything.”

“Then don't punish me for not giving you something.”

Her face changed.

Not much.

Enough.

After that, work became punishment delivered through policy.

His schedule changed without warning.

Good assignments moved to other people.

Minor mistakes became meetings.

Meetings became documentation.

Documentation became a performance plan.

He would correct one issue and discover three new expectations.

Coworkers who had once joked with him began lowering their voices when he approached.

A supervisor asked whether he had “personal instability affecting focus.”

He knew exactly where the phrase had started.

Or believed he did.

That difference would matter later.

A week after the photograph, his feed showed an advertisement about workplace affairs.

Another day, a song appeared whose chorus centered on jealousy and betrayal.

Vivian received an anonymous message containing cropped screenshots from Skyler's dating life.

Skyler received a screenshot showing that she had received them.

The Network—if it was the Network—did not need to invent jealousy.

It only needed to put a finger on a bruise.

What followed felt like torture to Skyler, though nobody laid a hand on him.

It was the bureaucratic kind.

The calendar moving.

Access changing.

Coworkers going quiet.

A meeting invitation arriving Friday at 4:47 p.m.

A badge still working while everybody in the building somehow knew it soon would not.

The final meeting happened behind the same blinds.

Ben sat beside Vivian and looked miserable.

HR joined by video.

The official explanation used words like **judgment**, **professionalism**, **disruption**, and **business needs**.

Nobody said jealousy.

Nobody said dancers.

Nobody said love.

Vivian barely looked at him.

“Is this your decision?” Skyler asked.

HR answered instead.

“This is a company decision.”

He kept looking at Vivian.

“Is it yours?”

Her jaw tightened.

“Skyler.”

“That's not an answer.”

For a moment he thought she might tell the truth.

Not the dramatic truth.

Just something human.

I cared.

I got jealous.

I let it affect how I treated you.

Instead she pushed his badge across the desk.

“We're done.”

He picked it up.

“You could have just hated my dating choices like a normal person.”

Ben coughed into one fist because laughter and horror sometimes share a doorway.

Vivian did not smile.

Skyler left carrying a cardboard box that contained almost nothing: a charger, a notebook, two pens, a mug somebody had given him, and the strange knowledge that affection could become hostility without ever changing clothes.

At home he taped the third white rectangle to the wall.

For a long time he wanted to write one word on it:

**JEALOUSY.**

That word would have made the story clean.

Clean stories are dangerous when the evidence is messy.

So he wrote:

**Confirmed:** employment ended.

**Confirmed:** Vivian expressed personal feelings.

**Confirmed:** discipline increased afterward.

**Possible:** jealousy influenced how the conflict developed.

**Possible:** outside actors amplified a private bruise until it became a workplace crisis.

**Unknown:** which decisions would have happened anyway.

It was less satisfying than certainty.

It was also more human.

Vivian did not need to become a monster for the experience to hurt him.

And Skyler did not need to pretend he had handled every boundary perfectly in order to say that power had made the situation unsafe.

That distinction would eventually save him.

The fourth job ended in a carpeted office that smelled faintly of coffee and someone else’s cologne. The supervisor, a man with a handsome jaw and sincere eyebrows, placed Skyler’s access badge on the table between them as if returning a borrowed item rather than ending an agreement.

“There’s an allegation,” the supervisor said.

“An allegation of what?”

“That you have… I’m reading here… that you have been sharing proprietary information with foreign actors.”

Skyler looked at the badge. It had his photo and the company’s name in glossy letters that reflected the fluorescent light in trembling bands.

“What actors?” he said, his voice flat in a room that ate sound.

“It’s phrased generically.”

“That’s convenient.”

“I’m not the bad guy, Skyler,” the supervisor said gently. “This came from the outside. We’re required to respond.”

“If it came from the outside, who inside told them what to call me?”

The supervisor’s mouth opened and closed once, like a fish in an aquarium at the dentist’s office. “We’re—this is procedural.”

“You asked for my help hardening your login flows,” Skyler said. “I showed you where your third-party access could be abused by scripts that mimic an employee’s OAuth exchange. I saved you three compliance flags. Now I’m a foreign actor.”

“No one used the word spy,” the supervisor said.

“That’s a relief.”

They did not shake hands. In the hallway, a young man with a cracked-mirror sticker on his water bottle walked by without looking up. The sticker could have been a band logo. It could have been nothing. Chance had the exact shape of intention when a person could not sleep.

On the wall at home, the fourth rectangle widened the white space like teeth set around a throat.

The fifth job ended so quietly he did not know it had ended. His login failed with a red text he had written into other systems years ago: Authentication Error: Please contact your administrator. He sent two emails to an address labeled admin@, waited, checked a status page that said all services were operating. His invoice sat in sent limbo. Then, the company’s website, which had once displayed a smiling composite of contributors, rolled over in the night and woke without him. His photograph disappeared from the line of faces loading slow enough to watch.

When he called the main switchboard, he reached a hold queue that played an instrumental with a high hat that kept stuttering on the off-beat. He had never listened before for beats like this. Now his body flinched without permission.

On the wall, the fifth rectangle made a modest sound when the tape grabbed plaster.

He ate standing at the counter. He slept in two-hour hunks when sleep landed at all. Exhaustion whitened the edges of the world until the corners looked lit. He began to mistake that brightness for revelation. He would see a word twice in a day and feel its weight as if someone had set the repetitions there on purpose. He knew how the mind worked under pressure. He also knew what it felt like when systems took the cracks a person carried and widened them to the size of rooms.

The sixth job lasted nine days. He timed them. They were good days in a way that almost made him feel unfaithful to his fear. The job was at a free clinic on a wide street where old sycamores made a cathedral watercolor on the pavement. He was supposed to clean up their intake flow, less glamour than mercy. The rooms smelled like antiseptic and the kind of hand soap that tries to cheer up fluorescent lights.

A nurse named Ramon showed him the strange beauty of paper. “Our binder is faster than your system in a blackout,” he said, and Skyler believed him.

On day three, around lunch, a teenage girl with a thin braid cried into her hands behind the check-in window. Ramon said, “She’ll be okay. She thought she had something no one will love her for. Turned out she doesn’t.”

Skyler went outside and watched two boys with helmets too big ride bikes in slow circles. He thought of 2020, how dread had widened until it took the color out of fruit and laughter out of rooms, and how he had survived anyway. He had taken the girls every other weekend with a schedule the court made a machine of. He had been the man at a food pantry handing sacks into outstretched hands, the man asking an old man with a cough whether he had someone to check on him tomorrow. He had enrolled in a night class for systems design just to remember what structure felt like when it wasn’t a punishment. He had sung off-key into a cheap mic on Sunday mornings when Mariana’s keyboard drowned out the tremble in his breath. He had stood in the rain with Summer because she liked the way water softened the world into one big hush. He had drawn chess knights on napkins for Luna when dinner at a diner became their only kind of fancy. He had shown Alexis how to hold a cheap compass flat in her palm and told her, in the good way fathers are allowed to simplify the world, that north would wait for her to stop spinning.

He had called that time a cage while he was inside it. He could name it now as something else. An unchosen sabbatical into usefulness. An unfair sentence that nonetheless contained time he had not earned. He had repaired a railing on Maria’s porch and painted her small yellow kitchen the week she came home from church with her arm in a sling from slipping in the fellowship hall. He had learned that love is patience that doesn’t keep score.

On day nine at the clinic, his badge failed as he walked through the side entrance. The red light was bland as an apology.

Ramon met him in the hall. “I’m told to collect the laptop,” he said, wincing at the script.

“By who?”

“Our director. There’s a review.”

Routine review was a moth that kept finding the bulb.

“Did someone say I’m unsafe?”

Ramon looked at the floor. “They sent a PDF with your name and… things. Posts? I don’t know, man. I told them you fixed our form. People filled it wrong all year, and you made it idiot-proof.”

“If it were idiot-proof,” Skyler said, trying for tone, failing, “I wouldn’t have been able to fix it.”

They both almost laughed. Ramon’s hand hovered near Skyler’s shoulder in a way that asked permission and then withdrew without offense when none was given.

He went home on foot. The city did not make eye contact. At a corner store, a radio station moved from an ad about a mortgage product to a chorus about walking it off. He did not assume. He carried the nine days like a small fire he was afraid to set down.

On his apartment wall, he taped rectangle number six. The tape made a dull rip. He wrote nine days under the date, the way a parent might write how tall a child was when she stood beneath a doorframe.

Hanna came to see him that evening, the ninth day. She had changed her hair. It was shorter now, dark near the roots, with a slash of lighter brown like she had let the sun into it. She still stood near doors, an old habit that had never been about exit routes so much as boundary lines kept clear.

She wore a green coat and no phone in her hand. He knew the shape of her hand well enough to know the missing weight.

“You look terrible,” she said, without drama, as if she were saying the sky had stitched itself from cloud.

“People keep saying that.”

“People keep seeing you.”

He tried to smile. “I’m being watched.”

“Maybe.”

He studied the angle of her mouth. It had been the jury once. It held kindness like a parent holds a child who will not stop wriggling.

“I’m not taking sides in a word game,” she said when he did not speak. “It means I believe you are frightened. I believe some people may be using that fear. I do not know whether every interpretation you have is accurate.”

He looked past her at the wall of jobs. Seven rectangles was the future the wall promised. Only six had earned their paper so far. The seventh waited like a shape you could see in negative space.

“It feels like they took everything,” he said. “The house. Kaylee. The girls under my roof. Seven jobs. Relationship after relationship turning into another room where I expected the floor to move.”

“Who?”

He opened his mouth.

Nothing clean came out.

“I don’t know,” he said.

That answer hurt more than a name would have.

“Then why do you say they?” Her voice was soft enough that it wasn’t a challenge until he felt challenged.

“Because it can’t be nobody.”

“Maybe it is not one person,” Hanna said, stepping closer to the wall, leaving the door at her back without flair and without fear. “Maybe it is several people. Maybe one event caused another without anyone planning the whole thing. Maybe some decisions were ordinary and your mind is trying to make them form a shape.”

“Are you saying I’m imagining it?”

“No.”

“Are you saying it’s real?”

“I’m saying reality is not required to match the first explanation that gives you relief.”

The sentence made him angry because it sounded like care instead of agreement. Anger disguised as a hunger to be agreed with was not new in him. It had lost him as much as the Network ever would.

“Why did you come?” he asked.

“Because I wanted to see whether you were alive.”

“I’m alive.”

“That is not the same as being here.”

He met her eyes. They were clear. They filled but did not spill. He hated the way he loved her then.

“I cannot be your witness every hour,” she said. “I cannot prove your phone is compromised. I cannot monitor everyone who speaks to you. I cannot become another device you use to measure danger.”

He felt abandoned before she left. That was the weakness in him: he treated boundaries as doors closing, even when they were the only doors keeping love from becoming a cage.

“I’m trying to save my family,” he said. The sentence had come to define his mouth. He wished he could unsay it and replace it with specific names, with Luna’s knight, Summer’s rain, Alexis’s compass.

“I know,” she said.

“Then help me.”

“I am helping you by telling you the truth.”

The word truth had become a blade everyone carried.

Hanna touched the edge of the job list with her forefinger, a gesture that made him think of a nurse testing a bruise.

“Put a category beside each one,” she said.

“What category?”

“Confirmed. Possible. Unknown.”

“Did Lena tell you about the Protocol?” he asked.

“No. You did.”

He had no memory of telling her.

The room seemed to tilt, the way something tilts right before it falls, or right after you stand up too fast and the blood slows because it is arguing with gravity.

Hanna saw it happen. “Skyler.”

“Did I call you?” His voice was tin, too light.

“Yes.”

“When?”

“Yesterday.”

“What did I say?”

“You said, ‘They are making me look guilty.’”

“I said that?”

“Yes.”

“Did I sound drunk?”

“No.”

“Angry?”

“Afraid.”

He sat down because the chair arrived beneath him before he had decided to sit. Hanna did not touch him. She had learned him. She waited until he looked at the floor instead of the walls.

“Write it down,” she said.

He opened the notebook. He wrote while saying the words in his head in the voice he saved for statements that counted.

I called Hanna yesterday. I do not remember the call. She says I sounded afraid. This is confirmed by her account, but my memory is absent.

He added:

Memory is not a perfect witness. Neither is fear.

Hanna read the sentence upside down. “That is the first thing you’ve written that sounds like a way forward.”

After she left, he recorded an audio note because he did not trust his hand alone to carry weight.

“I used to think a person became ruthless when he stopped caring,” he said into the mic. “I was wrong. Sometimes ruthlessness is what caring becomes when it has no place to go.”

He listened to the recording once. The sound of his own voice had begun to feel to him like a neighbor he did not like but could not evict. He deleted the audio on purpose, an act of resistance against his own archive.

Three minutes later, his device buzzed. The deleted file had returned with a new title: THE WARRIOR IS READY.

The device offered him the option to play. He did not know whether to scream or laugh. He did neither. He wrote in the ledger:

The file returned. Cause unknown. Do not interpret.

He sat in the dark then, as if he could turn off light and muffle thought. The city outside was a tide he could not track. He closed his eyes and saw not the world but the wall.

On the morning that would mark the beginning of the seventh job and its near-instant ending, he woke to a text from Caleb: Look alive, man. Coffee? Then: Or is coffee surveillance now? He almost replied. He did not. He did not have the energy to endure the kindness after the joke.

Driftwork Labs was a start-up full of the kind of people who put lowercase letters in proper nouns on purpose. Their office was a white box stuffed with potted plants and a neon sign that said make it make sense. They made consent flows for subscription platforms, which felt like hiring an arsonist to teach fire safety and calling it reform.

A project manager named Jo met him in a huddle room with a glass door. She had a small tattoo on the inside of her wrist, a rook made by two black squares. He could not help thinking of Luna’s chessboard the night she beat him for the first time and tried so hard not to gloat that her mouth shook.

“We’ve wanted you for a while,” Jo said, smiling like people do when they are trying to be a safe person in a profession that consumes empathy for fuel. “We’ve heard… stories.”

“What kind of stories?”

“The kind where you see around corners.” She tapped the tablet in front of her. “We want to implement a version of your four—”

“Principles,” he said before he could stop himself. Identity, memory, consent, consequence. They weren’t magic. They were the only doors he trusted, and even then he had learned to check the hinges.

“Yeah. But keep it… softer.” She shuffled windows on her screen. “Some of our partners are nervous about the word consent. Isn’t that wild?”

“Wild,” he said.

He worked for three days on a map of permissions that would insist on human-readable explanations before any automated process acted in a user’s name. He put the word explanation where other people hid labels like experience. He made the diagram bright because people preferred bright to true.

On the fourth day, the CTO, a man whose beard looked like an art project about beards, asked if they could build a backflow, a graceful override that allowed top-tier partners to move past prompts when the prompts were too slow. Skyler said, “That’s like building a seat belt with an eject button for when you’re running late.” The CTO called his metaphor excessive. Jo looked tired.

On the fifth day, he found a printout on a desk in the kitchen with a heading sketched at the top in a hand that liked block letters: SKYLER BLUE, NOTES. Beneath it: warrior is ready. He picked up the paper. His vision dipped and rose. Someone had transcribed the title of the file that had returned itself to his device and turned it into a phrase at work like a hat he was meant to wear. He was not that warrior. He was a father who wanted quiet days with muffins and crayons and the ocean you can hear inside a shell bought at a store nowhere near a coast.

On the sixth day—less than a week—the head of legal sent him an email that did not include hello. It embedded a link and a sentence: Our partners flagged this and expressed concerns re reputational risk.

The link opened to a well-designed site with a banner that borrowed fonts he had used once for a pitch deck. The page said Truth Protocol in grayscale above an avatar of his own face cut out from a conference photo five years old. The site listed entries in a format disturbingly close to his own. Confirmed. Possible. Unknown. It had been styled with care, as if someone wanted the feeling of diligence without the substance.

He scrolled through entries that were not his. He found a line about the warehouse drop, written as if he had admitted directing the courier. He found phrases he recognized, repurposed to sell a story about him to a reader like Jo’s legal team. He found an embedded audio clip that began with his voice saying, “I used to think a person became ruthless…” and ended with a splice that cut to the phrase THE WARRIOR IS READY, as if he had declared war and the site were simply curating his heroism. He had never said those words in that order. The title had been written over his deletion like graffiti on a grave.

He did not click any link that offered to Show More. He knew how pages made other pages inside a person’s head.

He took a breath that tasted metallic. He pressed the phone to the desk hard enough that he heard the case creak. On the far end of the office, someone laughed. The ordinary contagious happiness of the sound hit him with an envy he could almost name without shame.

He did what he had taught other people to do. He turned on the screen recorder. He captured a slow scroll from top to bottom, including the URL bar, the time, the battery icon, the notification shade. He took his hand off the mouse and let the page sit long enough for the inactivity timer to pop a light dim in the corner. He narrated nothing, because narration would have turned evidence into editorial.

Then he wrote in the notebook:

Someone has created a fake version of the Truth Protocol using my name and format. It contains misquotes, false admissions, and entries I did not write. Confirmed: existence of site, capture with date/time. Unknown: originator, publisher, host owner, motive beyond obvious. Consequence: employment threatened, reputation manipulated.

He sent Jo a short reply: Please consider this fake. Here is my record, which I have not published. He did not attach the record. He no longer believed in sending anything into a room he could not see.

Ten minutes later, the head of legal wrote back: Given current environment and counsel advice, we are initiating a routine review of all contractors. We will follow up.

He scraped his chair back from the desk. He stared at the green EXIT sign above the door until the rectangle of brightness ached in his eyes. He collected his backpack like a reflex.

On his way out, he passed a corkboard where employees had pinned Polaroids with captions written in Sharpie: jo’s bday! first launch!! legal team karaoke. He stood there longer than the moment deserved because a small photo in the middle showed three kids at a picnic table bent over a paper map. The caption read: driftwork family day. One child held a cheap plastic compass above the page with the seriousness of a navigator. He saw Alexis’s hands in the photo even though the kids were not his kids. He closed his eyes and saw her waiting for the needle to settle. It was a cheap trick his brain played, turning strangers into daughters because he needed the shape of them to hold him still.

Driftwork did not send a follow-up that day. He understood their timing. Absence could be procedure.

He went home and added a seventh rectangle of blank paper to the wall. He did not write the date. He let the possibility hang like a punch that might yet land. He stood in front of the wall with a pen uncapped and did not touch the paper to the surface. He left ink to dry in air.

His temporary device vibrated. He almost didn’t look. Then he did. A message had arrived from an address he did not know. The subject line contained his name and the word RANSOM. He deleted the message unopened. He would not feed the Choir. He made himself breathe on a four-beat count: in, out, in, out, until the animal inside him stopped kicking at his ribs.

He thought of calling Maria. He pictured how she would answer in the soft register she reserved for bad news and births. He knew what she would say: Eat something. Go outside. Do not decide alone. Pray, but do not think prayer is a lever.

Instead he texted Lena a single sentence: Somebody built a fake Protocol with my voice. He attached the screen capture. He hesitated and added: I’m not publishing until we verify.

Then, because no action could complete him, he stood and moved the plant from the windowsill to the kitchen sink and filled the sink with water until the pot refused another ounce. He watched the bubbles rise through the dry dirt and pop soundlessly.

At dusk, rain arrived without warning and stitched the street in silver. He stepped into it and lifted his face like people do in movies when they are forgiven. He was not forgiven. He was wet. He thought of Summer, how she would open her mouth to taste the sky and then laugh at the way water felt like a hand trying to comb her hair. He wanted to call her and ask what the rain was saying where she was. He did not call because he refused to make her day another exhibit.

He went back in, ate a sandwich standing up, wiped crumbs from his shirt with the back of his hand like a teenager, and sat down to do the only honest thing he could do. He drew three columns beside the job rectangles. He wrote Confirmed in one, Possible in another, Unknown over the last. He began to move small truths into boxes where they belonged and left large temptations to explain the world where they could not hurt anyone.

Under the seventh empty space he wrote, in tiny letters he almost hoped he couldn’t read: Do not let their version of you become your version of you.

His device buzzed. He looked at it as a person might look at an animal that has bitten before. A new post had gone up on the fake Protocol. It used the same grayscale header. It contained one line in quotation marks attributed to him.

“The warrior is ready.”

He hadn’t said it. It didn’t matter. The quote box did not care about origin. It asked only to be framed and shared. Beneath, a count ticked up. Fifty-one shares. Fifty-six. Sixty-three.

He closed the screen.

He opened the notebook.

The record can protect you, he wrote, echoing Hanna. But it can also become a room with no door.

He underlined the sentence and slid the notebook under a stack of folders as if putting it beneath paper might transform it from trigger to tool.

He did not sleep the way people sleep in movies, one scene cut to the next with morning already in it. He slept by inches. He woke twice to a truck braking outside. He woke a third time to the thought that had hunted him for a month: He was both seen and unseen. He was both chosen and discarded. He either had to become the person the page said he was or he had to learn how to stand in front of the page without setting it on fire.

At 2:10 a.m., he sent the link to Reyes with a three-word message that left no room for theater: Need chain validation. Then he set the phone face down and stared at the blank seventh rectangle until his own reflection in the dark window twinned it.

Morning would bring coffee that tasted less like coffee and more like memory. Morning would bring a bleeding of light along the apartment’s seam that told the day it was allowed to start. Morning would also bring, he knew without knowing how, an answer from someone in a suit who would use words like review and integrity and the names of procedures that had never been built for scenes like this.

Before he closed his eyes, he pictured his daughters’ faces one by one and told them quietly in the room, as if they were under his roof: I am here. I am working. I am not a warrior. I am your father.

The fake site’s counter ticked in the dark, indifferent. The rain idled on. Somewhere, as if the city had a composer who enjoyed a motif, a car passing on the street played four notes and let the last one hang too long. He registered the sound, wrote nothing about it, and chose not to decide what it meant. That, more than anything else that night, felt like a change.

## Chapter Twelve
### The Evidence

The Truth Protocol began as a folder on a borrowed laptop.

By the time Skyler named it, it had become a discipline.

The room Lena had rented was above a used bookstore that smelled like paper baked by summers. Someone had painted the walls the color of cheap cream once, and the daylight that came through the tall windows made the scuffs look like a map of old countries. A box fan rattled in one window. A radiator the size of a small tomb was bolted beneath the sill and promised heat it would never again deliver.

Downstairs, a bell chimed every time a customer opened the door. The sound traveled through the boards, a muffled punctuation to conversations about rare travelogues and poetry paperbacks and whether the register would agree to run a card today.

Lena had paid cash and asked no questions. She had come with two rolling suitcases, a bin of legal pads, and a tape measure. Before anyone set a laptop on the table, she taped off an area on the long walnut slab and labeled the edges in six-inch increments with blue painter’s tape. “Perspective correction later,” she’d said. “No debates about whether the photo made the phone look closer than it was.”

Reyes had provided a small audio recorder that blinked a patient red. “Do not narrate unless you agreed in advance what to say,” he warned over the line. “Observations, not arguments.” His voice came through tinny and careful. There were interruptions: the scratch of a chair on linoleum, someone in his office asking for a form, a printer spitting. Even away from them, bureaucracy hummed.

Mara had shown up with a canvas satchel, a fresh dressing on the graze along her ribs, and a stack of printed forms from the Mirror Room, grids that made Skyler’s stomach go cold. Name. Last known employer. Known associates. Political exposures. Family. Trigger inventory. The categories were clinical and obscene, like a hospital questionnaire rewritten by a magician with a grudge.

“Caleb’s bringing food,” Lena said, as if calories might fog the sharpness in the room.

“He’s also bringing a timer,” Caleb said, when he arrived with paper bags that valved steam into the dusty light. “You get forty minutes of flow and then fifteen minutes to pretend you sleep like a normal person. I will throw soup at anyone who fights me.”

“It’s a rental,” Lena said from the floor, where she crouched aligning three power strips with tape so the cords would run parallel. “Let’s not coat their antique floorboards in pho.”

They placed the evidence on the long wooden table.

Screenshots. Receipts. Audio files. Public posts. Employment emails. Photographs. Call logs. A photograph of the church package with the faint gold letterhead that had said, Blessing Kit. A photograph of the dark-blue sedan idling too long outside his building, pixels breaking the sheen on the trunk into a puzzle that refused to resolve. The four printed identity cards the stranger had placed by his mailbox slot like playing cards — each with his name mangled and a different photograph that belonged to no one he knew.

The material looked unimpressive.

That was the first lesson.

Fear made every object look connected. Verification made most objects smaller.

Lena divided the table into three sections using torn folders as fences. She wrote headings with a black marker in block letters and pressed the paper so hard the imprint ghosted the table.

CONFIRMED.

POSSIBLE.

UNKNOWN.

Mara objected to the categories.

“Possible is too broad,” she said. Her voice still had the smoked rasp of someone who had spent nights telling herself not to cry.

“It is honest,” Lena replied, not looking up.

“It contains everything.”

“Exactly.”

Skyler found himself hovering between them, like a misbehaving child choosing which parent’s house he would rather be grounded in. He wanted certainty. He also did not trust his wanting.

He placed the first message under confirmed: YOU FINALLY NOTICED. He had the screenshot; he had the time written on a napkin from the night it had arrived, because he had been afraid to save it anywhere else. The napkin felt ridiculous on the table next to legal paper and battery-backed clocks, and that was part of the point. He didn’t get to curate the past to make it handsome.

Beside it, he set the dark-blue sedan advertisement, printed with the date at the top and the ad code in the corner that meant almost nothing to a man who had once understood code as a language of order. He wrote on the sticky that tagged it: confirmed as advertisement; unknown as targeted. He wanted to write, It felt like a message, but feelings didn’t get a category.

The speaker recording — the one that had continued for nine seconds after he had pulled the power, the one that had made the hair on his arms rise like magnetic filings — went under confirmed as an audio file that outlasted a cord. The cause he left as unknown. He could hear Maria’s voice even now: electricity does weird things, mijo. He had watched her reset a fuse box with the same decisiveness she used to reset a child.

The warehouse photograph showing the car found near a building assigned to a federal task force — an image ripped from a late-night news site — went under confirmed as a broadcast. Its relationship to the message the laundromat stranger had delivered by hand remained possible.

He put the fake political post under confirmed as a thing that had been shared by Skyler, because he refused the formulation passive voice allows a guilty man. Unknown as to its origin. Unknown as to whether the scheduling Lena had found in his account settings had been his hands or another’s. The post itself, a bland exclamation about “taking our country back” that had no country in it when read with cold eyes, looked small on printer paper. It was easier to be ashamed of impulses when they had been pixel-bright. On paper, the embarrassment was duller, and somehow truer.

The church package — the one that had made his building’s lobby smell like potpourri and card stock and had made his neighbor with the small dog frown at him with new math in her eyes — went under confirmed as a delivery, unknown as to the sender. His mother had half-joked the day it arrived, “People used to leave casseroles after a death,” and then had put on her reading glasses to examine the return address that said only a city and a post office number.

The red-cap man went under possible.

Skyler hated placing him there. He hated the compromise of the word. He hated the night that had come with it.

“Why not confirmed?” he asked, because stubbornness is a muscle he had honed when softer muscles failed.

“Because you cannot confirm that he was the same person from 2020,” Lena said without apology.
